COMLEX-USA L1 Answer Keys and Explanations

Numbered to match the practice tests in your printed study guide.

Practice Test 1 Answer Key

  1. The correct answer is C (Neisseria gonorrhoeae causing disseminated infection with the classic triad of tenosynovitis, dermatitis and arthritis): Disseminated gonococcal infection in a sexually active young adult produces migratory polyarthralgia, tenosynovitis and pustular or vesicular skin lesions, often with a mildly inflammatory effusion. Staphylococcus aureus typically causes a single, markedly purulent joint. Rheumatic fever and Lyme disease lack the tendon sheath and pustular findings described.
  2. The correct answer is B (Inhibition of calcium release through the ryanodine receptor of skeletal muscle sarcoplasmic reticulum): This is malignant hyperthermia from a volatile anesthetic and succinylcholine, treated with dantrolene, which blocks the ryanodine receptor and stops uncontrolled calcium release in skeletal muscle. Blocking acetylcholine receptors would not stop calcium release inside the muscle fiber, and the hyperthermia is peripheral rather than a raised hypothalamic set point.
  3. The correct answer is B (Segmental facilitation at these levels sustains sympathetic outflow, raising heart rate and vascular tone): Sympathetic fibers to the heart and vessels arise from T1 to T5, and a chronically facilitated segment can lower the threshold for sympathetic discharge, which fits the tachycardia, raised pressure, and ropy tissue of long-standing dysfunction. Reduced vagal outflow would be suggested by cranial base findings, which are not present here.
  4. The correct answer is E (Naloxone has a shorter duration of action than the opioid, so receptor blockade wore off): Naloxone is a competitive antagonist whose clinical effect lasts a short time, whereas long-acting opioids such as methadone persist far longer. As naloxone is cleared, the remaining opioid again occupies mu receptors and depresses respiration, so patients need observation or repeat dosing. It is not an agonist and does not upregulate receptors.
  5. The correct answer is B (Alpha-2-delta subunit of presynaptic calcium channels, reducing excitatory neurotransmitter release in the dorsal horn): Pregabalin binds the alpha-2-delta subunit of presynaptic voltage-gated calcium channels, decreasing release of glutamate, substance P, and norepinephrine in the dorsal horn. Duloxetine acts at monoamine transporters, carbamazepine at sodium channels, opioids at mu receptors, and NSAIDs at cyclooxygenase, so each is a tempting but different mechanism.
  6. The correct answer is D (Statistical power falls, raising the chance of a type II error beyond the intended level): Fewer participants than planned reduces statistical power, which raises the probability of a type II error, so a real difference may have been missed. Alpha is set in advance, so the type I error rate is unchanged. A nonsignificant result does not prove the null hypothesis, and sample size itself does not create selection bias.
  7. The correct answer is D (Osteosarcoma or other sarcoma arising from the abnormal bone, presenting as new focal pain and swelling): Normal calcium, phosphate, and gamma-glutamyl transferase with a high alkaline phosphatase of bone origin and a cotton wool skull indicate Paget disease of bone. Sarcomatous transformation, usually osteosarcoma, is the feared complication in long standing disease. Myeloma and prostate metastases can mimic the biochemical picture but are not complications of Paget disease.
  8. The correct answer is B (Squamous cell carcinoma, with a higher rate of recurrence and metastasis in transplant recipients): Chronic immunosuppression and sun exposure markedly raise squamous cell carcinoma risk, which outnumbers basal cell carcinoma in transplant recipients and behaves more aggressively. A rapidly growing keratin-filled crater is the classic appearance. Keratoacanthoma-like lesions in this setting are treated as squamous carcinoma, and Merkel cell tumors are usually smooth violaceous nodules.
  9. The correct answer is C (Supraspinatus tendon and subacromial bursa pinched between the humeral head and acromion): A painful arc between 60 and 120 degrees and a positive Hawkins test indicate subacromial impingement, in which the supraspinatus tendon and bursa are compressed under the acromial arch. Full strength argues against a significant rotator cuff tear. Biceps, labral, subscapularis, and nerve lesions each have a different provocation pattern.
  10. The correct answer is B (Chronic anterior uveitis, which is often painless and silent until synechiae or cataract develop): Oligoarticular juvenile idiopathic arthritis with positive antinuclear antibody carries the highest risk of chronic anterior uveitis, which is usually asymptomatic and can lead to synechiae, band keratopathy and cataract. This is why guideline based slit-lamp screening is done at scheduled intervals even when the eyes look normal. Scleritis is a rheumatoid arthritis complication in adults.
  11. The correct answer is C (Pleural pressure equalizes with atmospheric, so unopposed elastic recoil of the lung pulls it inward): Normally the lung recoils inward while the chest wall recoils outward, which holds intrapleural pressure below atmospheric. A ruptured subpleural bleb lets air enter the pleural space, so the pressure equalizes and the lung collapses. Hyperresonance with absent breath sounds distinguishes this from an effusion or mucus-plug atelectasis.
  12. The correct answer is D (Substituted judgment, choosing what he would decide given his known values and past statements): The surrogate hierarchy starts with substituted judgment, reconstructing what the patient himself would choose from his values, and the proxy knows them well. The objective benefit and burden standard applies only when his wishes cannot be inferred. Her own preference is irrelevant to the decision.
  13. The correct answer is C (Normal pressure hydrocephalus with impaired cerebrospinal fluid absorption producing the gait, urinary, and cognitive triad): The triad of magnetic gait, urinary urgency, and cognitive slowing with ventriculomegaly out of proportion to atrophy identifies normal pressure hydrocephalus, which may improve after cerebrospinal fluid drainage. Alzheimer disease usually preserves gait until late, and Parkinson disease would show tremor and respond to levodopa.
  14. The correct answer is E (Aspergillus fumigatus, a mold with septate hyphae branching at acute angles that invades pulmonary vessels): Prolonged neutropenia is the major risk factor for invasive aspergillosis, which causes angioinvasive nodules with a halo sign and releases galactomannan detectable in serum. Septate hyphae with acute-angle branching distinguish it from Rhizopus, which has broad nonseptate hyphae and is typical in diabetic ketoacidosis. Yeasts and Pneumocystis do not produce this angioinvasive pattern with a positive galactomannan.
  15. The correct answer is D (Comfort-focused hand feeding, since tube feeding has not been shown to prolong survival in advanced dementia): In advanced dementia, feeding tubes do not reduce aspiration, prevent pressure injury or prolong life, and they add risks such as restraint use and infection. Careful hand feeding preserves comfort and contact and fits goals of care discussed with the surrogate. Absence of a directive does not oblige artificial nutrition.
  16. The correct answer is E (A fusion protein with constitutive tyrosine kinase activity driving unchecked proliferation of myeloid precursors): The Philadelphia chromosome, t(9;22), joins BCR to ABL1 and produces a fusion tyrosine kinase that is constitutively active, causing chronic myeloid leukemia with marked leukocytosis and a low leukocyte alkaline phosphatase score. This is the target of imatinib. The 14;18 and 11;14 translocations belong to lymphomas, and 15;17 belongs to acute promyelocytic leukemia.
  17. The correct answer is A (Intravenous sodium bicarbonate, which raises serum pH and sodium load to relieve sodium channel blockade): A wide QRS and terminal R wave in aVR show sodium channel blockade from a tricyclic, which causes arrhythmias and hypotension. Sodium bicarbonate gives sodium and alkalinizes the blood, reducing drug binding to the channel. Physostigmine and class IA antiarrhythmics are contraindicated, and charcoal alone is inadequate for a toxic patient.
  18. The correct answer is D (Low-flow injury at the watershed between superior and inferior mesenteric artery territories): The splenic flexure lies at the watershed between the superior mesenteric artery and the inferior mesenteric artery, so systemic hypotension produces segmental ischemic colitis there while the rectum is spared by its dual internal iliac supply. An acute proximal superior mesenteric embolus would infarct small bowel as well.
  19. The correct answer is A (Perifascicular fiber atrophy with inflammatory infiltrates concentrated around perimysial vessels and connective tissue septa): Heliotrope eyelids, Gottron papules and proximal weakness indicate dermatomyositis, a humoral microangiopathy. Biopsy shows perifascicular atrophy with perimysial and perivascular inflammation, unlike polymyositis, where CD8 cells invade fibers within the endomysium.
  20. The correct answer is B (Tubular obstruction and oxidant injury from filtered myoglobin, worsened by volume depletion): Dark urine that is heme positive on dipstick without red cells points to myoglobinuria from rhabdomyolysis. Myoglobin obstructs tubules, generates ferryl heme oxidant injury and constricts afferent vessels, especially with volume depletion and acidic urine. Hemolysis would also give heme pigment but does not fit the exertional setting with muscle breakdown, hyperkalemia and hypocalcemia.
  21. The correct answer is A (Restrictive barrier, a pathologic limit that reduces motion inside the usual normal range): A restrictive barrier is an abnormal limit that shortens motion within the normal range, which is what distinguishes it from the physiologic barrier that ends normal active motion. The anatomic barrier lies beyond all of these and is the limit at which tissue damage begins, so it cannot describe an early firm stop.
  22. The correct answer is A (Granuloma macrophages express 1-alpha-hydroxylase and convert vitamin D to calcitriol without feedback regulation): Erythema nodosum, hilar adenopathy and noncaseating granulomas indicate sarcoidosis. Activated macrophages in the granulomas express 1-alpha-hydroxylase, producing calcitriol outside the kidney without the normal feedback control. This raises calcium and suppresses parathyroid hormone, unlike primary hyperparathyroidism.
  23. The correct answer is B (Reversible competitive antagonism at the same binding site, overcome by higher agonist concentration): A parallel rightward shift with an unchanged maximum indicates that the compound competes with the agonist for the same site, so more agonist restores the full response and potency falls. Noncompetitive or irreversible antagonists lower the maximum because functional receptors are lost. A partial agonist would also lower the ceiling.
  24. The correct answer is E (Returning him to neutral too abruptly restarted the abnormal proprioceptive reflex activity): A quick return from the position of ease can restimulate the spindle and bring the tenderness back, so the patient should be returned to neutral slowly and passively. The hold of about ninety seconds was adequate. Hypertension does not block the effect, and the technique does not use compression.
  25. The correct answer is A (Methylene blue to reduce ferric iron in hemoglobin back to the ferrous state): Dapsone oxidizes hemoglobin iron to the ferric state, forming methemoglobin that cannot carry oxygen and gives chocolate brown blood with a normal PaO2. Methylene blue is reduced by NADPH and then reduces methemoglobin back to functional hemoglobin. Cyanide antidotes target cytochrome oxidase poisoning, a different mechanism.
  26. The correct answer is C (Lisinopril, which lowers fetal angiotensin II and impairs glomerular filtration and urine production): ACE inhibitors cross the placenta in the second and third trimesters and lower fetal angiotensin II, reducing glomerular filtration. The resulting oligohydramnios causes pulmonary hypoplasia, Potter-like facies and calvarial hypoplasia. Atenolol can limit fetal growth but does not abolish urine output, and warfarin produces bony and nasal malformations from early exposure.
  27. The correct answer is D (Right torsion, named for the side of the superior greater wing of the sphenoid): Torsion is named for the side of the higher greater wing of the sphenoid, here the right, with the occiput rotating the opposite way around the anteroposterior axis. Sidebending rotation is named for the side of convexity and occurs around vertical axes. Lateral and vertical strains involve translation of the sphenoid base rather than opposite rotation of the greater wings.
  28. The correct answer is A (Deliberate feigning of symptoms driven by an external goal such as drugs or compensation): Conscious fabrication or exaggeration for a clear external reward, here opioids and disability benefits with legal involvement, defines malingering, which is not a psychiatric disorder. Factitious disorder is also deliberate but lacks external incentive, while conversion and somatic symptom disorder are not consciously produced.
  29. The correct answer is A (Loss of prostaglandin mediated afferent arteriolar dilation, lowering glomerular filtration during volume depletion): In a volume depleted patient, afferent arteriolar dilation by prostaglandins preserves glomerular filtration, and cyclooxygenase inhibition removes it. Combined with an ACE inhibitor that dilates the efferent arteriole and a diuretic, this produces the so called triple whammy of prerenal injury. Interstitial nephritis is possible but does not fit this hemodynamic setting best.
  30. The correct answer is E (Numbness of the thumb, index and middle fingers with normal sensation over the thenar eminence): Compression beneath the flexor retinaculum affects the median nerve's digital branches to the lateral three and a half digits, but the palmar cutaneous branch arises proximally and passes superficial to the retinaculum, so thenar skin stays normal. Loss of thenar skin sensation would suggest a lesion proximal to the wrist.
  31. The correct answer is B (Junction of the left subclavian and left internal jugular veins posterior to the clavicle): The thoracic duct drains the lower body and the left upper body and empties at the left venous angle, so fascial restriction at the thoracic inlet can impede its outflow. The cisterna chyli is the origin of the duct rather than its termination, which makes it a tempting but incorrect site here.
  32. The correct answer is D (Failure of the aorticopulmonary septum to spiral during division of the truncus arteriosus): Transposition of the great arteries arises when the aorticopulmonary septum fails to spiral, creating parallel circulations that need a mixing shunt such as a patent duct to survive. Prostaglandin E1 keeps the ductus open and briefly improves saturation. Tetralogy of Fallot also causes cyanosis but shows decreased pulmonary markings and a boot-shaped heart.
  33. The correct answer is A (Activating mutation of a cytoplasmic kinase causing growth factor independent signaling in marrow progenitors): A high hematocrit with a low erythropoietin level, splenomegaly, thrombocytosis, aquagenic pruritus and erythromelalgia indicates polycythemia vera, driven by a JAK2 V617F mutation that makes progenitors independent of erythropoietin. Secondary causes would show high or inappropriately normal erythropoietin. Normal oxygen saturation also argues against hypoxic drive.
  34. The correct answer is E (L2 to L4 neutral, rotated left, sidebent right, a type I group dysfunction): A neutral group curve follows type I mechanics, with sidebending and rotation to opposite sides, and the dysfunction is named for the directions of freedom. Free right sidebending with free left rotation therefore gives neutral, rotated left, sidebent right. A neutral group is not a single flexed or extended segment, so the type II designations do not fit.
  35. The correct answer is D (Short duration of the reversal drug relative to the opioid, permitting renewed mu receptor activation): Naloxone is a competitive antagonist with a short duration of action, while methadone acts for a day or longer, so receptors are re-occupied as the antagonist wears off. This is why patients need observation or an infusion, and the recurrence of the same opioid picture argues against a new sedative.
  36. The correct answer is C (Schizophreniform disorder, since psychotic symptoms have lasted over one month but under six months): The symptoms meet the criteria for schizophrenia but have lasted three months, so schizophreniform disorder is the diagnosis until six months have passed. Brief psychotic disorder ends within a month, and schizoaffective or delusional disorders do not fit absent mood episodes and with prominent hallucinations.
  37. The correct answer is D (Covalent alpha-receptor blockade that lowers the maximal response by reducing the functional receptor pool): Phenoxybenzamine binds alpha receptors covalently, so receptors are removed from the available pool and the maximal response falls; extra agonist cannot overcome this. A reversible competitive blocker would only shift the curve rightward. Phenylephrine acts directly, so depletion of stored norepinephrine would not blunt it.
  38. The correct answer is A (Placental thromboplastin entering the circulation, driving systemic thrombin generation and factor consumption): Placental abruption releases tissue factor-rich material into the maternal circulation, causing disseminated intravascular coagulation with consumption of fibrinogen and factors, prolonged PT and aPTT, and a high D-dimer from secondary fibrinolysis. Hepatic failure would not raise D-dimer so markedly, and a lupus anticoagulant causes thrombosis rather than oozing.
  39. The correct answer is D (Insufficient surfactant from immature type II pneumocytes causing high alveolar surface tension and atelectasis): Prematurity and maternal diabetes (fetal hyperinsulinism delays surfactant synthesis) predispose to neonatal respiratory distress syndrome. Without surfactant from type II cells, surface tension collapses alveoli, giving atelectasis, a ground-glass pattern with air bronchograms and low lung volumes. Transient tachypnea and meconium aspiration are disorders of term infants.
  40. The correct answer is D (Parainfluenza virus causing subglottic edema and a narrowed airway below the vocal cords): Barking cough, hoarseness and inspiratory stridor after a cold in a toddler describe croup, caused most often by parainfluenza virus infecting the subglottic region. Epiglottitis causes drooling and toxicity, RSV causes lower airway wheezing, and diphtheria and pertussis have distinctive membranes or whooping paroxysms.
  41. The correct answer is B (Increased tubular hydrogen secretion and ammonium excretion, with new bicarbonate generation over several days): Over three to five days, proximal tubule ammoniagenesis and distal hydrogen secretion rise, generating new bicarbonate that returns the pH close to normal in chronic respiratory acidosis. Cell buffering raises bicarbonate only slightly and within minutes, so it cannot account for a bicarbonate of 37 mEq/L.
  42. The correct answer is E (Acute respiratory alkalosis with a widened alveolar-arterial gradient from perfusion mismatch and increased dead space): The pH is high with a low carbon dioxide and normal bicarbonate, an acute respiratory alkalosis. Estimating the alveolar oxygen at roughly 112 mm Hg and subtracting the measured 62 gives a gradient near 50, which is wide for his age and fits embolic perfusion mismatch rather than hyperventilation alone. Pain driven hyperventilation would keep the gradient normal.
  43. The correct answer is D (Defective DNA mismatch repair allowing microsatellite instability and rapid accumulation of mutations): Early proximal colon cancer with endometrial cancer in relatives and loss of MSH2 and MSH6 staining indicates Lynch syndrome from defective mismatch repair, which causes microsatellite instability. APC loss causes florid polyposis, and the sporadic KRAS and p53 sequence does not explain loss of repair protein staining.
  44. The correct answer is B (Cauda equina syndrome from a large central disc herniation compressing the lumbosacral nerve roots): Severe bilateral radicular pain, saddle anesthesia, urinary retention, reduced rectal tone and absent ankle reflexes indicate compression of many lumbosacral nerve roots, a surgical emergency. Conus lesions tend to cause less radicular pain and more symmetric early sphincter loss, and myelitis would produce a trunk sensory level and spasticity.
  45. The correct answer is C (Urinary antigen assay detecting Legionella pneumophila serogroup 1 lipopolysaccharide shed into the urine): Pneumonia with diarrhea, headache, confusion, and hyponatremia after exposure to aerosolized water points to Legionella, which stains poorly and does not grow on routine blood agar. The urinary antigen detects serogroup 1, the most common cause, and is the quick confirmatory test. The pneumococcal urinary antigen is tempting because the format is the same, but it would not identify this organism.
  46. The correct answer is A (Sensitivity, the true positive rate among all persons who actually carry the disease): Sensitivity is the proportion of truly diseased persons who test positive, so it equals the true positive rate. Specificity describes the healthy group instead, and predictive values depend on the tested population's prevalence, so neither answers a question conditioned on disease status.
  47. The correct answer is E (Ascorbic acid deficiency, impairing hydroxylation of proline and lysine needed for stable collagen cross-linking): Bleeding gums, perifollicular hemorrhages, corkscrew hairs, and impaired wound healing in a man without fruit or vegetables indicate scurvy. Vitamin C is a cofactor for prolyl and lysyl hydroxylase, and its lack weakens collagen. A normal prothrombin time and platelet count argue against a vitamin K deficiency, and zinc deficiency does not cause these follicular findings.
  48. The correct answer is D (The left ventricle depolarizes first, and the right ventricle follows late by slow spread): In right bundle branch block the septum and left ventricle activate normally, giving the initial small r in V1, and the right ventricle depolarizes late by cell-to-cell spread, causing the terminal R' in V1 and the wide S in lateral leads. Left bundle branch block reverses the sequence and the septal vector, producing broad R waves in the lateral leads.
  49. The correct answer is D (Viral E6 promoting degradation of p53 while E7 binds retinoblastoma protein to release E2F): High-risk HPV types express E6, which targets p53 for ubiquitin mediated degradation, and E7, which binds the retinoblastoma protein and frees E2F to drive the cell cycle. Together they remove two key checkpoints. Reversing the targets of the two proteins is the most tempting error.
  50. The correct answer is C (Persistence past six months reflects delayed cortical inhibition of a brainstem reflex and needs workup): The Moro reflex is a brainstem-mediated primitive reflex that normally disappears by about four to six months as cortical control matures. Persistence at nine months suggests delayed inhibition from cerebral injury or dysfunction. The parachute reflex is easily confused with it, but that reflex appears later and persists for life, and it involves protective forward arm extension when the infant is tipped.
  51. The correct answer is E (Low serum C3 with subepithelial immune complex humps on electron microscopy): Nephritic syndrome with red cell casts two weeks after pharyngitis fits poststreptococcal glomerulonephritis, which shows low C3 and subepithelial humps from immune complexes. IgA nephropathy follows an infection within a day or two and keeps complement normal. Membranoproliferative disease also lowers C3 but lacks the clear infectious trigger and humps.
  52. The correct answer is B (Crossing axons from nasal retinal ganglion cells decussating within the optic chiasm): Nasal retinal fibers carry the temporal visual field and cross in the chiasm, which lies directly above the pituitary. A suprasellar mass compresses these crossing fibers and produces bitemporal hemianopia. Lateral compression, such as from a carotid aneurysm, affects uncrossed temporal retinal fibers instead.
  53. The correct answer is C (Folic acid, which supports DNA synthesis in rapidly dividing embryonic cells during early gestation): Folic acid begun before conception and continued into early pregnancy reduces the risk of neural tube defects by supporting thymidylate and purine synthesis in rapidly dividing cells. High dose vitamin A is teratogenic. Iron and vitamin D are useful in pregnancy but do not prevent failure of neural tube closure.
  54. The correct answer is A (Loss of E-cadherin expression weakening epithelial cell to cell adhesion and permitting dispersed stromal spread): Invasive lobular carcinoma characteristically loses E-cadherin, so tumor cells lose cell to cell cohesion and infiltrate as single files without forming a mass. This explains the subtle distortion on imaging. HER2 amplification and TP53 loss are associated with ductal tumors that form cohesive masses.
  55. The correct answer is D (Diphenhydramine, a sedating anticholinergic antihistamine that raises delirium, constipation, and fall risk in older adults): First-generation antihistamines such as diphenhydramine are on the Beers list because anticholinergic and sedative effects cause confusion, constipation, dry mouth, and falls. His symptoms match these effects. The other drugs are reasonable in this setting, and sleep hygiene is the safer approach to insomnia.
  56. The correct answer is E (Cranial nerve X preganglionic fibers from the medulla oblongata synapsing in the bowel wall): The vagus nerve carries cranial parasympathetic outflow to the gut through about the splenic flexure, which is why suboccipital and upper cervical work is used for upper and midgut function. Sacral outflow is also parasympathetic but supplies the distal colon. Cranial nerves VII and IX target head glands, not the bowel.
  57. The correct answer is D (Colonic bacterial breakdown acidifies stool, trapping ammonia as ammonium and accelerating its fecal elimination): Lactulose is broken down by colonic bacteria into acids, lowering stool pH so that ammonia is converted to ammonium, which cannot diffuse back into the blood and is excreted. The laxative effect also shortens transit. Rifaximin works by reducing ammonia producing bacteria, which is the tempting wrong mechanism here.
  58. The correct answer is E (Continuous positive airway pressure at night, which splints the collapsing upper airway during sleep): Moderate to severe obstructive sleep apnea is treated first with continuous positive airway pressure, which keeps the pharyngeal airway open, and with weight loss, which lowers collapsibility. Wakefulness agents only treat sleepiness. Sedative hypnotics can worsen obstruction, and oral appliances are mainly for mild to moderate disease or intolerance of pressure therapy.
  59. The correct answer is B (Spores surviving cooking germinate in slowly cooled food and release enterotoxin in the gut): Clostridium perfringens spores survive cooking and germinate when large batches cool slowly, and the ingested cells sporulate in the gut and release an enterotoxin that causes watery diarrhea after about 8 to 16 hours. Staphylococcal toxin is preformed, so illness starts within a few hours with prominent vomiting. Salmonella causes fever and a longer incubation.
  60. The correct answer is C (Hypoventilation, because retained carbon dioxide displaces alveolar oxygen and the gradient stays normal): Alveolar oxygen is about 0.21 times 713 minus PaCO2 divided by 0.8, which is roughly 150 minus 88, or 62 mm Hg. Subtracting the measured PaO2 of 55 gives a gradient near 7 mm Hg, which is normal. Hypoxemia with hypercapnia and a normal gradient therefore reflects hypoventilation from opioid-induced respiratory depression.
  61. The correct answer is B (Volume repletion lowers angiotensin II and aldosterone, allowing bicarbonate excretion with chloride delivery distally): Vomiting loses gastric acid and volume, and the volume contraction raises angiotensin II and aldosterone, which keep reabsorbing bicarbonate and secreting hydrogen despite the alkalosis. A low urine chloride marks this saline-responsive state. Isotonic saline restores volume and chloride delivery so the kidney can excrete bicarbonate; potassium repletion helps but is not supplied by saline.
  62. The correct answer is B (Dysgerminoma, the ovarian counterpart of testicular seminoma, usually sensitive to radiation and platinum therapy): Sheets of large clear cells with fibrous septa and lymphocytes, with raised lactate dehydrogenase and normal alpha-fetoprotein and gonadotropin, identify a dysgerminoma. It is the ovarian equivalent of seminoma and is radiosensitive. A yolk sac tumor would be recognized by raised alpha-fetoprotein and Schiller-Duval bodies.
  63. The correct answer is A (Anterior cruciate ligament, which limits the shin sliding in front of the thigh bone): The Lachman test, performed at about 20 to 30 degrees of flexion, assesses anterior tibial translation, which the anterior cruciate ligament chiefly restrains. A soft endpoint and a pivoting mechanism with rapid hemarthrosis support a tear. The posterior cruciate ligament limits posterior translation, and the collaterals are tested with varus and valgus stress.
  64. The correct answer is E (Excess antidiuretic hormone increasing free water reabsorption in collecting ducts with persistent urinary sodium loss): Euvolemic hyponatremia with concentrated urine and urine sodium above 40 mEq/L in a smoker with a central mass points to ectopic ADH from small cell carcinoma. Water is retained, which dilutes serum sodium, while natriuresis persists. Adrenal insufficiency can look similar but requires a different clinical context, and heart failure would show edema with low urine sodium.
  65. The correct answer is D (Masking treatment assignment from participants and investigators using a look-alike inactive tablet): A subjective outcome in an unmasked trial is vulnerable to expectation effects in both patients and clinicians. Double blinding with an indistinguishable placebo directly targets this problem. A larger sample improves precision but not bias, and intention-to-treat analysis protects against attrition and crossover rather than perception bias.
  66. The correct answer is B (Coronary artery aneurysm from medium vessel vasculitis, with a risk of thrombosis or infarction): Kawasaki disease is a medium vessel vasculitis, and the coronary arteries are the most important target, so echocardiography at diagnosis and in follow-up detects aneurysms that can thrombose. Rheumatic mitral disease follows streptococcal infection and develops over years rather than in the acute febrile illness.
  67. The correct answer is A (The body can regulate itself and has an inherent capacity to heal when supported): Supporting hydration, rest, and drainage while the body recovers reflects the tenet of self regulation and self healing. The fourth tenet is a summary of the other three and the preventive principle is worthwhile but is not a tenet.
  68. The correct answer is E (Pleomorphic adenoma, the most common benign salivary neoplasm, which carries recurrence risk if enucleated): A painless, slowly growing, mobile parotid mass with a mixture of epithelial and myoepithelial cells in a myxochondroid stroma is a pleomorphic adenoma. Simple enucleation can leave tumor pseudopodia and lead to recurrence. Warthin tumor has lymphoid stroma, and the carcinomas lack the chondroid stroma and often cause nerve findings.
  69. The correct answer is D (Type III mechanics, where initiating movement in one plane alters the range available in the others): Type III describes how motion introduced in one plane modifies the available motion in the remaining planes, which matches the finding that sidebending first changes rotation and flexion. The symmetric neutral testing argues against a type I group pattern, and a type II pattern requires a segment held in flexion or extension.
  70. The correct answer is B (Squamous cell carcinoma, promoted by impaired immune surveillance of ultraviolet damaged keratinocytes in sun exposed skin): Chronic immunosuppression and ultraviolet damage greatly increase squamous cell carcinoma, which outnumbers basal cell carcinoma in transplant recipients, the reverse of the general population. Kaposi sarcoma and Merkel carcinoma are also increased but are much rarer, which is why sun protection and regular skin examination are emphasized.
  71. The correct answer is C (Give parenteral thiamine first, since carbohydrate metabolism consumes the cofactor and can worsen neuronal injury): This is Wernicke encephalopathy (confusion, ophthalmoplegia, ataxia) from thiamine deficiency in alcohol use disorder. Thiamine is a cofactor for pyruvate dehydrogenase and transketolase, so a glucose load given first can exhaust remaining stores and worsen mammillary body injury. Hypoglycemia still needs treatment, but thiamine goes before or with it.
  72. The correct answer is C (Secure the airway in the operating room with anesthesia and otolaryngology present): Abrupt toxic fever, drooling, muffled voice and the tripod posture in an unvaccinated child indicate epiglottitis, a rapidly obstructing airway emergency. The airway should be secured under controlled conditions by experts, and any agitation, throat examination or transport for imaging risks sudden obstruction. Racemic epinephrine and steroids treat croup, which has a barking cough instead.
  73. The correct answer is B (MDMA, a serotonin releasing amphetamine that causes hyperthermia, bruxism and excess antidiuretic hormone release): Intense emotional closeness, bruxism, hyperthermia, seizure and hyponatremia point to MDMA, which releases serotonin and promotes antidiuretic hormone secretion, and the large water intake worsens dilution. Cocaine causes hyperthermia and seizures but not an empathogenic state or hyponatremia. Phencyclidine typically produces nystagmus and violence rather than dilutional sodium loss.
  74. The correct answer is C (Monoclonal light chains precipitating with Tamm-Horsfall protein to form obstructing casts in distal tubules): Multiple myeloma produces free light chains that dipsticks, which detect albumin, miss, while sulfosalicylic acid detects them. These light chains bind Tamm-Horsfall protein and form obstructing casts in distal tubules, causing cast nephropathy. Hypercalcemia can contribute but does not explain the Bence Jones proteinuria, and amyloid would give heavy albuminuria.
  75. The correct answer is D (Acquisition of mecA encoding an altered penicillin binding protein with low affinity for beta lactams): Methicillin resistant Staphylococcus aureus carries mecA on the SCCmec cassette, producing PBP2a, which has low affinity for nearly all beta lactams including oxacillin and cefazolin. Plasmid penicillinase explains penicillin resistance only, since oxacillin is penicillinase stable.
  76. The correct answer is B (Early adrenal androgen secretion without activation of the gonadal axis, known as premature adrenarche): Adrenarche is the rise of adrenal androgens that produces body odor and pubic or axillary hair without breast development. Normal growth velocity and near-normal bone age argue against central precocious puberty and hyperplasia. The pattern is benign, though it warrants follow-up for later progression.
  77. The correct answer is E (Autoantibodies stimulating the TSH receptor, driving unregulated synthesis and release of T3 and T4): Diffuse enlargement with a bruit and infiltrative eye disease is typical of Graves disease, caused by IgG antibodies that stimulate the TSH receptor. Hormone output is unregulated and diffusely increased. A toxic adenoma and thyroiditis do not cause ophthalmopathy.
  78. The correct answer is A (An SSRI such as sertraline, started low and titrated, for sustained reduction of attack frequency): Panic disorder is treated long term with an SSRI or SNRI plus cognitive behavioral therapy, starting at a low dose because early jitteriness can aggravate attacks. A benzodiazepine can bridge the first weeks but is not sound maintenance therapy because of tolerance, withdrawal and misuse risk. Buspirone helps generalized anxiety but is not effective for panic.
  79. The correct answer is C (Defective LDL receptor function, reducing hepatic clearance of LDL particles from plasma by receptor-mediated endocytosis): Tendon xanthomas, corneal arcus, premature coronary disease in a relative and isolated LDL elevation indicate familial hypercholesterolemia from defective LDL receptors, so LDL clearance falls. Normal triglycerides argue against remnant disorders, lipoprotein lipase problems and insulin-resistant VLDL overproduction. Abetalipoproteinemia gives the opposite pattern with very low LDL.
  80. The correct answer is A (Hepatitis C virus, since chronic infection is associated with oral and cutaneous lichen planus): The flat-topped violaceous papules with Wickham striae in the mouth are lichen planus, which has a recognized association with chronic hepatitis C infection. Secondary syphilis, guttate psoriasis and scabies can cause itchy or scaly eruptions but do not give polygonal violaceous papules with lacy oral changes.
  81. The correct answer is E (Group AB plasma, because it lacks both anti-A and anti-B antibodies that attack recipient cells): Plasma carries the donor's antibodies, so the safest plasma for an unknown recipient is the one with no anti-A or anti-B, which is group AB. Group O plasma contains both anti-A and anti-B and would hemolyze group A, B or AB recipient cells. This is the reverse of the red cell rule, where group O is the universal donor.
  82. The correct answer is B (QT interval prolongation with risk of torsades de pointes on electrocardiogram): Ondansetron is a 5-HT3 antagonist that can prolong the QT interval, and sotalol adds additional QT prolongation through potassium channel blockade, raising the risk of torsades. The other effects belong to dopamine antagonists, antimuscarinics or opioids.
  83. The correct answer is B (Oligoclonal immunoglobulin G bands in spinal fluid, with increased intrathecal antibody synthesis): Optic neuritis, internuclear ophthalmoplegia, symptoms worsening with heat, and periventricular lesions disseminated in time and space indicate multiple sclerosis. Oligoclonal IgG bands in spinal fluid but not matched in serum support the diagnosis. Albuminocytologic dissociation points to Guillain-Barre syndrome instead.
  84. The correct answer is D (Place a chest tube for continuous drainage, since this fluid is a complicated parapneumonic effusion): A pleural pH below 7.2, low glucose, and high lactate dehydrogenase indicate a complicated parapneumonic effusion approaching empyema, which requires drainage with a chest tube because antibiotics alone fail. Repeat sampling delays the necessary drainage. Pleurodesis is for recurrent malignant effusions, and steroids do not clear loculated infected fluid.
  85. The correct answer is A (NF2 on chromosome 22 encoding merlin, a membrane cytoskeletal tumor suppressor protein): Bilateral vestibular schwannomas with an affected parent and spinal tumors define neurofibromatosis type 2, caused by loss of the NF2 gene on chromosome 22. Neurofibromatosis type 1 is on chromosome 17 and causes neurofibromas, café-au-lait spots, and optic gliomas, not bilateral eighth nerve tumors.
  86. The correct answer is C (Transition to a safer antiseizure drug before conception and begin higher dose folic acid supplementation): Valproate carries the highest neural tube defect risk among common antiseizure drugs, and the neural tube closes around day 28, often before pregnancy is recognized. The risk is reduced by changing to a lower risk agent and supplementing folic acid before conception. Switching in the second trimester arrives long after the vulnerable period has passed, and stopping all medication risks uncontrolled seizures.
  87. The correct answer is D (Stomach, because abdominal lymph drains upward via the thoracic duct into the left venous angle): A hard left supraclavicular node, known as Virchow node, drains lymph from the thoracic duct, which collects abdominal lymph. With early satiety, weight loss, and iron deficiency anemia, an occult gastric adenocarcinoma is most likely. Head and neck primaries drain to higher cervical levels, and the symptoms point away from a lung or thyroid source.
  88. The correct answer is A (Perifascicular atrophy with perivascular inflammation composed of B cells and CD4 T cells): Heliotrope rash and Gottron papules identify dermatomyositis, a complement mediated microangiopathy of the perimysial vessels that leaves perifascicular atrophy and perivascular inflammation by B and CD4 T cells. The endomysial CD8 infiltrate is characteristic of polymyositis, which lacks skin findings. Rimmed vacuoles occur in inclusion body myositis in older patients.
  89. The correct answer is A (Persistent sympathetic facilitation from cardiac disease, producing fibrotic texture in the upper thoracic segments): Chronic visceral disease leads to long-standing facilitation that evolves from warm, boggy tissue to cool, dry, fibrotic, ropy tissue at the cardiac segments, T1 to T5. Acute infarction would give warm, moist, tender tissue, which is the tempting mistake. Tissue texture, rather than diuretic use or a single rib, reflects the duration and visceral source of the reflex.
  90. The correct answer is B (Case fatality, 2 percent, from deaths among cases divided by the number of cases): Case fatality uses only affected patients as the denominator: 4 deaths among 200 cases is 2 percent. The mortality rate of 8 per 100,000 divides the same deaths by the whole town, and the attack rate describes how many became ill. A survival proportion of 98 percent is the complement, not the requested measure.
  91. The correct answer is E (Perifascicular atrophy with perimysial inflammatory infiltrate of CD4 positive T cells and B cells): The heliotrope rash and Gottron papules identify dermatomyositis, a complement mediated microangiopathy of muscle. Biopsy shows perifascicular atrophy with perimysial and perivascular inflammation. The endomysial CD8 pattern marks polymyositis, and rimmed vacuoles mark inclusion body myositis.
  92. The correct answer is B (Tuberculosis, since silica uptake injures alveolar macrophages and weakens their control of inhaled mycobacteria): Silicosis causes upper lobe nodules and eggshell calcification of hilar nodes. Silica is engulfed by alveolar macrophages and damages them, which impairs killing of mycobacteria and raises the risk of tuberculosis. Mesothelioma and pleural plaques are linked to asbestos.
  93. The correct answer is A (Loss of pain and temperature, since spinothalamic fibers cross close to their level of entry): This is Brown-Sequard syndrome from a left hemisection. Motor and dorsal column loss are ipsilateral, while spinothalamic fibers cross within a segment or two of entry, so the opposite leg loses pain and temperature below the level. Corticospinal fibers cross in the medulla, so weakness is on the side of the lesion.
  94. The correct answer is D (Chapman reflex points, neurolymphatic tissue changes that reflect visceral dysfunction and guide diagnosis and treatment): Chapman reflex points are small, firm, pea-sized neurolymphatic tissue changes in deep fascia with paired anterior and posterior locations, and they reflect visceral dysfunction. Trigger points and counterstrain points sit in muscle and tendon, facilitation describes a spinal mechanism rather than the palpable nodule, and acupuncture points belong to a different system.
  95. The correct answer is C (Anti-D immune globulin at 28 weeks gestation and within 72 hours of the first delivery): An Rh-negative mother with anti-D antibodies and a positive direct antiglobulin test in an Rh-positive infant, with matching ABO types, indicates Rh hemolytic disease from sensitization during the first Rh-positive pregnancy. Anti-D immune globulin at 28 weeks and within 72 hours after delivery clears fetal red cells before the mother mounts an immune response. Treatments for fetal anemia occur only after sensitization.
  96. The correct answer is E (Caspase-3, an executioner protease activated by cleavage that digests nuclear and cytoskeletal proteins): Councilman bodies are apoptotic hepatocytes: shrunken, eosinophilic, with fragmented nuclei and no inflammation. Caspase-3 is the executioner caspase that cleaves cellular proteins and activates DNases. Granzyme B from the T cell starts the cascade by activating procaspases, but it is not the final effector, and Bcl-2 would oppose death.
  97. The correct answer is A (Hypomagnesemia from reduced intestinal absorption, impairing parathyroid hormone secretion and tissue response): Long-term acid suppression is associated with hypomagnesemia, which blunts parathyroid hormone release and action and causes hypocalcemia and hypokalemia that resist replacement until magnesium is corrected. Calcium carbonate absorption does fall with higher gastric pH, but that would not explain the low potassium or tetany. Autoimmune and renal causes lack supporting findings.
  98. The correct answer is C (Loss-of-function mutation of filaggrin, impairing keratin filament aggregation and epidermal water retention): Infantile atopic dermatitis with a personal and family atopic history is linked to filaggrin loss-of-function mutations, which weaken the stratum corneum and let water escape and allergens enter. Pemphigus, ichthyosis and epidermolysis bullosa involve different proteins, and Malassezia drives seborrheic dermatitis rather than extensor eczema.
  99. The correct answer is A (Factor VIII deficiency, reducing assembly of the intrinsic tenase complex on activated platelets): An isolated prolonged aPTT that corrects on mixing, with a normal PT, normal platelet count, normal bleeding time and a markedly low factor VIII activity, in a boy with hemarthroses and an affected maternal uncle, indicates X-linked hemophilia A. Factor IX would give the same clinical picture but its activity is normal here. Von Willebrand disease would prolong bleeding time and rarely causes joint bleeds.
  100. The correct answer is B (Positive predictive value falls sharply and negative predictive value rises slightly as prevalence drops): Positive and negative predictive values depend on prevalence even when sensitivity and specificity are fixed. At very low prevalence most positive results are false positives, so positive predictive value collapses, while nearly everyone is truly healthy so negative predictive value approaches its maximum.

Practice Test 2 Answer Key

  1. The correct answer is D (Tidal volume near 6 mL/kg predicted body weight with plateau pressure below 30 cmH2O): The patient meets criteria for moderate to severe ARDS, and lung-protective ventilation with low tidal volumes and limited plateau pressure lowers mortality by reducing ventilator-induced lung injury. Nitric oxide improves oxygenation briefly but not survival, and oscillatory ventilation did not help in trials. Higher PEEP is generally used rather than minimal PEEP.
  2. The correct answer is B (Measles, mumps and rubella vaccine, which contains replicating weakened viruses that induce durable immunity): Measles, mumps and rubella vaccine is a live attenuated combination given at 12 to 15 months. Hepatitis A, pneumococcal conjugate, acellular pertussis with toxoids and injected poliovirus vaccines are all inactivated or subunit products that cannot replicate.
  3. The correct answer is C (Nitric oxide release raising cGMP, relaxing venous smooth muscle, and lowering ventricular preload): Nitrates are converted to nitric oxide, which activates guanylate cyclase and raises cGMP, causing smooth muscle relaxation. At anginal doses the venous effect dominates, reducing preload and wall tension and therefore oxygen demand. The headache comes from dilation of meningeal vessels.
  4. The correct answer is D (Alopecia areata, a T cell mediated autoimmune attack on actively growing hair bulbs): A smooth, sharply demarcated round patch with tapering broken hairs and nail pitting points to alopecia areata, in which T cells attack anagen bulbs but spare stem cells so regrowth remains possible. Tinea capitis is the tempting alternative in a child but produces scale and inflammation.
  5. The correct answer is B (Interstitial edema thickening the interlobular septa along the peripheral lung adjacent to pleura): These are Kerley B lines, produced by edema fluid distending the interlobular septa at the lung bases, and together with cardiomegaly and cephalization they indicate cardiogenic pulmonary edema. Pleural plaques from asbestos are calcified, irregular and usually located on the diaphragm or lateral chest wall, and they do not accompany orthopnea and cardiac enlargement.
  6. The correct answer is C (Stevens-Johnson syndrome with widespread keratinocyte apoptosis from a delayed T cell mediated drug hypersensitivity): Mucosal erosions, fever, dusky targetoid lesions and under 10 percent detachment after starting an aromatic antiseizure drug indicate Stevens-Johnson syndrome. Scalded skin syndrome spares the mucosa and affects mostly children, while DRESS usually appears two to eight weeks later with eosinophilia and organ involvement.
  7. The correct answer is A (Hydroxylation of proline and lysine residues in the procollagen alpha chains): Vitamin C is the cofactor for prolyl and lysyl hydroxylases, so scurvy produces under-hydroxylated procollagen that cannot form a stable triple helix, causing bleeding gums and poor healing. Propeptide cleavage and glycosylation do not require vitamin C. Lysyl oxidase needs copper, as seen in Menkes disease.
  8. The correct answer is E (Review the order with him preoperatively and agree whether to suspend, modify or maintain it): Perioperative do-not-resuscitate orders call for a preoperative discussion: a capable patient decides which resuscitative measures he accepts during and after surgery. Automatic suspension and automatic continuation both ignore his autonomy, and his wife acts as surrogate only if he lacks capacity. Cancelling surgery is unnecessary.
  9. The correct answer is B (Give intravenous potassium chloride first and delay insulin until the serum level rises above 3.3 mEq/L): The anion gap is 29, confirming diabetic ketoacidosis, but total body potassium is depleted and the serum level here is low. Insulin shifts potassium into cells and could cause fatal arrhythmia, so potassium is replaced first until it exceeds 3.3 mEq/L. Bicarbonate is not indicated at this pH, and phosphate is replaced only in selected cases.
  10. The correct answer is B (Compression duplex ultrasonography of the left leg before any lower extremity manipulation is performed): Pregnancy is hypercoagulable, and unilateral, warm, tender calf swelling suggests deep vein thrombosis rather than benign dependent edema. Imaging comes first, because pumping, massaging or mobilizing the limb risks embolization. Treatment of the lymphatic system can resume once thrombosis is excluded.
  11. The correct answer is C (Autoantibodies against presynaptic voltage-gated P/Q-type calcium channels, a paraneoplastic effect of small cell carcinoma): Proximal weakness that transiently improves with exertion, hyporeflexia, autonomic symptoms such as dry mouth, and a lung mass indicate Lambert-Eaton myasthenic syndrome. Antibodies against presynaptic calcium channels reduce acetylcholine release, and small cell carcinoma is the usual paraneoplastic source. Myasthenia gravis has ocular fatigable weakness and postsynaptic antibodies.
  12. The correct answer is B (Loss of angiotensin II mediated efferent arteriolar constriction lowers glomerular capillary hydrostatic pressure): With bilateral renal artery stenosis, glomerular filtration depends on angiotensin II constricting the efferent arteriole to maintain pressure. An ACE inhibitor removes that constriction and filtration pressure collapses, producing acute kidney injury and, with sodium retention, pulmonary edema. Interstitial nephritis and tubular necrosis do not fit this timing and setting.
  13. The correct answer is B (Secretin from duodenal S cells, stimulating ductal bicarbonate secretion and reducing gastric acid output): Duodenal acid triggers S cells to release secretin, which neutralizes acid by driving bicarbonate secretion from pancreatic ductal cells and also decreases gastric acid secretion. Cholecystokinin is released by fatty acids and amino acids rather than by acid.
  14. The correct answer is C (Posttest probability is about 9 percent, found by converting to odds before applying ratio): Convert the pretest probability of 50 percent to odds of 1, multiply by the negative likelihood ratio of 0.1 to get odds of 0.1, then convert back to a probability of about 9 percent. Multiplying probabilities directly gives 5 percent, which is wrong because likelihood ratios apply to odds. The surgery history and lisinopril do not change the calculation.
  15. The correct answer is B (Right posterior limb of the internal capsule from lenticulostriate branch occlusion): A pure motor hemiparesis involving face, arm and leg equally, without sensory, visual or cortical signs, is the classic lacunar syndrome of the posterior limb of the internal capsule, where corticospinal fibers lie close together. Lenticulostriate branches are small penetrators vulnerable to hypertensive and diabetic vessel disease. Cortical or medullary lesions would add other deficits or spare the face.
  16. The correct answer is D (Superior segment of the right lower lobe bronchus, which is most gravity dependent when recumbent): In a recumbent child the superior segment of the right lower lobe bronchus is the most dependent and posterior branch, so aspirated material tends to enter it. The right main bronchus is wider, shorter and more vertical than the left, which is why aspiration favors the right side overall. The posterior basal segment is the usual site when the child is upright, so it fits this history less well.
  17. The correct answer is D (Progesterone driven rise in minute ventilation with renal bicarbonate compensation, a normal pregnancy adaptation): Progesterone increases respiratory center sensitivity so minute ventilation rises, lowering PaCO2 to about 30 mm Hg, and the kidneys lower bicarbonate to about 20 mEq/L to hold pH near 7.44. The normal oxygen saturation, PaO2 and heart rate argue against embolism or heart failure. Acute hyperventilation would not yet show a lowered bicarbonate.
  18. The correct answer is E (Limited external validity, because the study population differs from the clinic's patients in key characteristics): Randomization, blinding and full follow-up protect internal validity, so the result is probably valid for the people studied. The clinic's concern is whether it generalizes to older and less motivated patients, which is external validity. Restricting enrollment narrows the population to which the findings reliably apply.
  19. The correct answer is B (Discontinue the offending anticoagulant and begin a direct thrombin inhibitor such as argatroban): A platelet fall of more than half between days 5 and 10 of heparin exposure with a new thrombosis is heparin-induced thrombocytopenia, an antibody-mediated prothrombotic state. All heparin must stop and a non-heparin direct thrombin inhibitor started. Warfarin alone can worsen thrombosis through early protein C depletion, and low molecular weight heparin cross-reacts with the antibodies.
  20. The correct answer is C (Alert the department chair immediately so the surgeon does not operate until he is evaluated): A physician's duty to protect patients requires immediate action when a colleague may be impaired, and the first step is to remove the patient from risk by alerting the chair or chief of staff so the surgeon does not operate. Private conversation or delayed reporting leaves the patient exposed. Board reporting may follow through institutional channels but does not stop this case.
  21. The correct answer is A (C3 to C5, the cervical segments contributing the motor fibers to the diaphragm): The phrenic nerve, the sole motor supply to the diaphragm, arises from C3 to C5, so dysfunction there can reduce excursion. The thoracic sympathetic levels, the upper cervical vagal region, and the lumbar crural attachments are real structures but do not provide the diaphragm's motor innervation.
  22. The correct answer is A (Intravenous calcium gluconate, which rapidly raises ionized calcium and reduces neuromuscular excitability after parathyroid injury): Perioral paresthesias, carpal spasm, a Chvostek sign, and QT prolongation after neck surgery indicate symptomatic hypocalcemia from parathyroid devascularization or removal. Intravenous calcium gluconate corrects it within minutes and protects against arrhythmia. Calcitriol and parathyroid hormone replacement are useful for maintenance but do not act quickly enough, and a bisphosphonate would lower calcium further.
  23. The correct answer is B (Noninvasive bilevel positive pressure ventilation to rest fatigued respiratory muscles and reduce hypercapnia): An alert, cooperative patient with a COPD exacerbation and acute hypercapnic acidosis (pH 7.28, PaCO2 68) benefits from bilevel noninvasive ventilation, which lowers the work of breathing, reduces intubation and improves survival. Uncontrolled high-flow oxygen can worsen carbon dioxide retention, and intubation is reserved for failure of noninvasive support. Bicarbonate does not fix the underlying ventilatory failure.
  24. The correct answer is B (Cerebral aqueduct, causing noncommunicating obstruction between the third and fourth ventricles): Enlarged lateral and third ventricles with a normal fourth ventricle place the block at the aqueduct, and downward gaze deviation (sunsetting) reflects pressure on the midbrain tectum. Blockage at the fourth ventricle outlets or failed absorption would also enlarge the fourth ventricle.
  25. The correct answer is D (Collapse of the pharyngeal airway from reduced upper airway muscle tone during sleep): Obstructive apnea is defined by absent airflow despite persistent respiratory effort, which points to upper airway collapse as pharyngeal muscle tone falls during sleep; obesity and hypertension are typical associations. Central apnea would show no effort at all, and a Cheyne-Stokes pattern is seen in heart failure or after stroke. Obesity hypoventilation causes sustained daytime hypercapnia rather than discrete apneas.
  26. The correct answer is B (Dantrolene, which blocks ryanodine receptor release of calcium from the sarcoplasmic reticulum): Malignant hyperthermia results from uncontrolled calcium release through ryanodine receptor type 1 after exposure to volatile anesthetics or succinylcholine, causing sustained contraction, heat production and rhabdomyolysis. Dantrolene binds the receptor and reduces calcium release from the sarcoplasmic reticulum. Bromocriptine is used in neuroleptic malignant syndrome and cyproheptadine in serotonin syndrome, which have different triggers.
  27. The correct answer is B (Spinal cord segments S2 to S4 through the pelvic splanchnic nerves reaching the hindgut): The hindgut distal to the splenic flexure receives parasympathetic supply from S2 to S4 through the pelvic splanchnic nerves, whereas the vagus supplies the gut only to about the splenic flexure. Lumbar splanchnic fibers are sympathetic and slow motility instead. The greater splanchnic nerve carries sympathetic fibers to the celiac ganglion.
  28. The correct answer is E (Partner with local agencies on shelter, nutrition and public transit to change conditions that shape health): Upstream, or primary prevention, interventions change social determinants such as housing, food access and transportation, which drive much of the gap in life expectancy. Screening and extended hours are secondary or tertiary measures that address disease after it appears. Patient education targets individual behavior without changing the conditions that limit it.
  29. The correct answer is D (Valsartan, an angiotensin receptor blocker that avoids ACE inhibition and bradykinin accumulation, with close monitoring): ACE inhibitor angioedema is bradykinin mediated, so an angiotensin receptor blocker is the usual replacement, used with caution. Sacubitril valsartan is contraindicated after angioedema because neprilysin inhibition also raises bradykinin. Another ACE inhibitor repeats the mechanism, and aliskiren and spironolactone do not replace the proven therapy.
  30. The correct answer is A (MEN type 2B from RET mutation, with mucosal neuromas, marfanoid habitus, and early medullary thyroid carcinoma): Mucosal neuromas of the lips and tongue, intestinal ganglioneuromatosis causing constipation, marfanoid habitus, and a family history of thyroid cancer and adrenal tumors point to multiple endocrine neoplasia type 2B. Type 2A shares the RET mutation and the tumors but does not produce mucosal neuromas or marfanoid habitus. Marfan syndrome has no endocrine tumors.
  31. The correct answer is D (Ferrochelatase and ALA dehydratase, blocking iron insertion into protoporphyrin and porphobilinogen formation): Pica or paint exposure, microcytic anemia with normal ferritin, basophilic stippling and lead lines on long bone radiographs indicate lead poisoning. Lead inhibits ferrochelatase and ALA dehydratase, so protoporphyrin and aminolevulinic acid accumulate. The normal ferritin separates this from iron deficiency, and stippling reflects ribosomal RNA retained by inhibited pyrimidine 5-nucleotidase.
  32. The correct answer is B (Reduced ankle jerk with weak plantar flexion and numbness along the lateral foot): A posterolateral L5 to S1 herniation compresses the traversing S1 root, which supplies plantar flexion, the ankle jerk and the lateral foot. Weak great toe dorsiflexion would indicate L5 root compression from the L4 to L5 level, and saddle numbness suggests a large central herniation affecting several roots.
  33. The correct answer is B (Binding FKBP12 and inhibiting calcineurin, which prevents NFAT dependent cytokine transcription within activated T cells): Topical tacrolimus binds FKBP12, and the complex inhibits calcineurin so NFAT cannot move to the nucleus to drive cytokine genes, giving steroid-sparing anti-inflammatory action without skin atrophy. Cyclosporine uses cyclophilin and sirolimus acts on mTOR, and neither is used as a topical dermatitis ointment.
  34. The correct answer is A (The noncollagenous domain of the alpha-3 chain of type IV collagen in basement membranes): Pulmonary hemorrhage with rapidly progressive glomerulonephritis and linear IgG staining is anti-glomerular basement membrane disease, caused by antibodies to type IV collagen. Smoking and infection expose the alveolar antigen. ANCA vasculitis also causes a pulmonary-renal syndrome but shows little or no immunoglobulin on staining.
  35. The correct answer is B (Anterior two thirds of the thoracic cord supplied by the anterior spinal artery): Aortic clamping reduces flow through the artery of Adamkiewicz into the anterior spinal artery, which supplies the anterior two thirds of the cord, including the corticospinal and spinothalamic tracts and anterior horns. Motor paralysis, pain and temperature loss and bladder dysfunction follow, while the posterior columns, fed by paired posterior spinal arteries, preserve vibration and position sense.
  36. The correct answer is D (Root cause analysis, a retrospective systems review that traces contributing latent factors behind an adverse event): Root cause analysis is a retrospective, systems-focused review of an adverse event, here the look-alike vials that made the error likely. Failure mode and effects analysis looks similar but is prospective, used before harm occurs. Peer review targets an individual clinician's performance, not the process.
  37. The correct answer is A (Supportive care with cautious fluid management and renal monitoring, and dialysis only if indicated): Shiga toxin producing E. coli O157:H7 causes hemolytic uremic syndrome, and management is supportive because antibiotics may increase toxin release and antimotility agents prolong exposure. Platelet transfusion can feed microthrombi and is reserved for active bleeding or procedures. Plasma exchange targets TTP, which lacks the diarrheal prodrome and renal predominance seen here.
  38. The correct answer is B (Combined oral contraceptive therapy suppressing ovarian androgen output while giving regular endometrial shedding): In polycystic ovary syndrome without a wish for pregnancy, combined oral contraceptives give predictable withdrawal bleeding, protect the endometrium, and lower ovarian androgen production and free testosterone. Progestin alone covers the endometrium but not the hirsutism. Spironolactone helps hair growth but needs contraception and does not regulate cycles.
  39. The correct answer is C (Salter Harris type II, which carries a favorable outlook after anatomic reduction): A fracture through the physis that exits through the metaphysis, leaving a triangular Thurstan Holland fragment, is Salter Harris type II, the most common type. It does not cross the epiphysis, so the germinal layer is usually spared and prognosis is good. Types III to V involve the epiphysis or crush the growth plate and carry higher risk.
  40. The correct answer is E (Blockade of low-threshold T-type calcium channels in thalamic relay neurons that drive oscillating cortical rhythm): Staring spells with generalized 3 Hz discharges indicate childhood absence epilepsy, for which ethosuximide is the drug of choice. It acts by blocking T-type calcium currents in thalamic neurons that generate the oscillation. Sodium channel blockers such as carbamazepine and phenytoin can aggravate absence seizures.
  41. The correct answer is D (Passive shortening of the tissue reduces abnormal proprioceptor input, held about ninety seconds): Jones proposed that a strained muscle's spindle sends persistent abnormal signals, and passively shortening the tissue for about ninety seconds quiets them. Stretching, active contraction and thrust belong to other techniques. Counterstrain is gentle, so compression or a thrust at the tender point would defeat its purpose.
  42. The correct answer is A (Exposure and outcome are measured together, so which came first cannot be determined here): A cross-sectional survey measures exposure and outcome at the same moment, so it cannot show that vaping came before wheezing. Reverse causation or shared causes remain possible. Random sampling helps generalizability but does not remove confounding, and no follow-up exists, so loss to follow-up is not a concern.
  43. The correct answer is B (Cryoprecipitate, a pooled product rich in factor I, factor VIII, and factor XIII): After massive obstetric bleeding the critical deficit is fibrinogen, which is only mildly supplied by plasma. Cryoprecipitate concentrates fibrinogen, factor VIII, factor XIII and von Willebrand factor in a small volume and raises fibrinogen quickly. The platelet count and INR are only mildly abnormal and do not explain the ongoing bleeding.
  44. The correct answer is B (Rosacea, a chronic inflammatory disease with vascular hyperreactivity and papulopustules centered on convex facial areas): Episodic flushing with identifiable triggers, persistent central erythema, and papulopustules without comedones define papulopustular rosacea. Lupus produces a fixed photosensitive malar rash without pustules, and acne vulgaris would show comedones.
  45. The correct answer is A (Cervical extension with small sidebending and rotation adjustments until tenderness decreases): Posterior tender points are treated with extension, with small sidebending and rotation adjustments to find the position of greatest comfort. Flexion is used for anterior points. Traction, a barrier-directed hold and a thrust are not counterstrain.
  46. The correct answer is E (Emphysema, with parenchymal destruction reducing elastic recoil and alveolar surface for gas transfer): An FEV1/FVC of 0.52 with increased total lung capacity and a scooped expiratory limb shows obstruction with hyperinflation. The reduced diffusing capacity points to loss of alveolar-capillary surface, which is characteristic of emphysema. Chronic bronchitis causes obstruction with a preserved diffusing capacity and prominent sputum production.
  47. The correct answer is B (Acknowledge his frustration, apologize for the delay, and invite him to explain what concerns him most): With a loud but non-threatening patient, the first step is empathic acknowledgment and an apology for the delay, which de-escalates and uncovers the real concern. Explaining, defending or shortening the visit escalates conflict. Threatening to call security is for genuine safety risk, and the examination follows once he feels heard.
  48. The correct answer is A (Community health worker, a trusted lay liaison who provides outreach, navigation and culturally concordant support): Community health workers come from the community they serve, build trust and address nonclinical needs such as transportation, insurance paperwork and home follow-up, which are this patient's barriers. A pharmacist or nurse care manager would address clinical management that is already adequate. An interpreter solves only language during encounters.
  49. The correct answer is C (Withhold osteopathic manipulation and obtain urgent noncontrast head CT for possible intracranial hemorrhage): Headache, vomiting, and drowsiness after head trauma in an anticoagulated patient suggest intracranial hemorrhage, so urgent noncontrast CT is the priority. Cranial osteopathic treatment is contraindicated in acute intracranial bleeding and raised intracranial pressure. Treating first or imaging later delays diagnosis and could worsen the outcome.
  50. The correct answer is E (Ultrasound-guided fine needle aspiration of the nodule, with cytology to classify malignancy risk): With a normal TSH and a suspicious sonographic appearance in a nodule over 1 cm, fine needle aspiration is the next step. A scan is reserved for a suppressed TSH where a hot nodule is possible. Surgery follows cytology, and suppression therapy has little role.
  51. The correct answer is B (Irreversible inhibition of gastric and pancreatic lipases, so undigested triglycerides pass into the colon): Orlistat covalently inhibits gastric and pancreatic lipases, so about 30 percent of dietary triglyceride is not hydrolyzed and passes to the colon, causing oily spotting and flatulence. The same steatorrhea reduces absorption of fat soluble vitamins, which is why a multivitamin is separated from the dose. Acarbose acts on carbohydrates and would not cause oily stool.
  52. The correct answer is C (A COL3A1 mutation producing structurally abnormal type III collagen in vessel walls): Translucent skin, spontaneous bowel perforation and early arterial rupture indicate vascular Ehlers-Danlos syndrome from COL3A1 mutations. Classic type shows hyperextensible skin and atrophic scars with COL5A1 defects but rarely arterial rupture. Normal height, arm span and eyes argue against Marfan syndrome, and fractures would point to type I collagen.
  53. The correct answer is E (Acquisition of mecA encoding an altered penicillin-binding protein with reduced affinity for beta-lactam drugs): Post-influenza necrotizing pneumonia with clustered gram-positive cocci is Staphylococcus aureus, and nafcillin resistance defines methicillin resistance. The mecA gene encodes penicillin-binding protein 2a, which beta-lactams bind poorly, so the cell wall still forms. Plasmid beta-lactamase explains penicillin resistance, but nafcillin is designed to resist that enzyme.
  54. The correct answer is E (Reassurance with dietary modification and soluble fiber, then symptom targeted therapy for the predominant complaint): Pain relieved by defecation with altered stool form, normal inflammatory markers, and no alarm features meets the criteria for irritable bowel syndrome, which is managed with reassurance, diet and fiber, and symptom directed drugs. Further testing is reserved for alarm features, older age, or failure of therapy.
  55. The correct answer is B (Impaired mutase conversion of an odd chain fatty acid metabolite, yielding abnormal myelin lipids): Cobalamin is a cofactor for methylmalonyl-CoA mutase and for methionine synthase, so deficiency raises both methylmalonic acid and homocysteine. Folate deficiency raises homocysteine only, and the neurologic injury of cobalamin deficiency is linked to the mutase defect and abnormal myelin lipids. Giving folic acid alone can correct the anemia while allowing neurologic damage to progress.
  56. The correct answer is B (Excess retinoid signaling that disrupts cranial neural crest cell migration and pharyngeal arch patterning): Isotretinoin is a vitamin A derivative whose excess retinoic acid signaling disrupts cranial neural crest cells, producing the microtia, cleft palate, conotruncal and thymic defects of retinoid embryopathy. Thalidomide also affects the ear but acts through cereblon to cause limb reduction, which is absent here. Folate antagonists chiefly cause neural tube defects.
  57. The correct answer is E (Dopamine D2 receptors in the nigrostriatal pathway, reducing inhibitory dopaminergic tone on striatal neurons): Metoclopramide is a D2 antagonist and 5-HT4 agonist that speeds gastric emptying, but D2 blockade in the nigrostriatal pathway causes acute dystonia, akathisia and parkinsonism. D2 blockade in the tuberoinfundibular pathway causes hyperprolactinemia, which is a different adverse effect.
  58. The correct answer is C (Deficiency of ankyrin or spectrin linking the membrane skeleton to the lipid bilayer): Spherocytes with high mean corpuscular hemoglobin concentration, a family history, and a negative antiglobulin test indicate hereditary spherocytosis, caused by defects in ankyrin, spectrin, band 3, or protein 4.2. Loss of membrane surface makes the cells spherical and vulnerable to splenic removal. Splenectomy reduces hemolysis.
  59. The correct answer is C (180 per 100,000 per year, the exposed rate minus the unexposed rate of disease): Attributable risk is the incidence in exposed persons minus the incidence in unexposed persons: 200 minus 20, or 180 per 100,000 per year, which counts preventable cases. The relative risk of 10 describes strength of association, not case numbers. The attributable proportion of 90 percent is a percentage and does not give cases per 100,000 smokers.
  60. The correct answer is D (Cortical destruction removing aldosterone and cortisol, with lost feedback raising pituitary POMC-derived melanocyte-stimulating peptides): Primary adrenal failure removes cortisol and aldosterone, causing hypotension, hyponatremia, hyperkalemia, and hypoglycemia. Falling cortisol removes negative feedback, so ACTH and its POMC cleavage products, including MSH, rise and darken the skin. Secondary insufficiency spares aldosterone and does not pigment.
  61. The correct answer is E (Short-term memory loss, including gaps for events near the time of the procedure): Psychotic depression with refusal of food and suicidal intent in an older adult is a strong indication for electroconvulsive therapy. Its most common adverse effect is transient confusion and memory impairment around the treatments, and it does not cause detectable structural brain damage or epilepsy.
  62. The correct answer is A (Loss of the paternally derived 15q11 to 13 region with the maternal copy silenced by imprinting): Hypotonia in infancy followed by hyperphagia, obesity, small hands and feet and mild intellectual disability is Prader-Willi syndrome. It arises from loss of the paternal 15q11 to 13 region, because the maternal genes are imprinted and silent. Angelman syndrome involves loss of the maternal copy and gives ataxia and inappropriate laughter.
  63. The correct answer is B (Intravenous calcium gluconate to raise the threshold for cardiac myocyte depolarization and stabilize the membrane): Peaked T waves, a widened QRS or flattened P waves signal hyperkalemic cardiac toxicity, and calcium gluconate antagonizes the resting membrane effect within minutes without lowering potassium. Insulin with glucose and albuterol then shift potassium into cells, and dialysis definitively removes it. A binder or diuretic is too slow or unreliable in a dialysis patient.
  64. The correct answer is D (Direct standardization, weighting each county's stratum rates by a common standard population structure): The crude gap reflects age structure, not different risk at each age. Direct standardization applies each county's rates to one shared reference population, removing the confounding effect of age. Case fatality and proportional mortality describe different quantities and do not adjust the population-wide rate.
  65. The correct answer is B (Nodular mesangial expansion with hyalinized arterioles and diffusely thickened glomerular basement membranes): Long standing diabetes with retinopathy and nephrotic proteinuria produces Kimmelstiel-Wilson nodules, hyalinosis of both afferent and efferent arterioles, and thick basement membranes. Amyloid is a reasonable alternative cause of nephrotic syndrome, but it would stain with Congo red and is not tied to retinopathy. Crescents and humps indicate nephritic syndromes.
  66. The correct answer is E (Functional technique, since the endpoint is judged by palpated tissue response): A nonverbal patient cannot give the tenderness ratings that counterstrain depends on, so a technique guided by the physician's palpation is more reliable. Functional technique is indirect and follows tissue response. Muscle energy, articulatory and high velocity low amplitude techniques are direct or active and do not meet the request for an indirect method, and counterstrain, although indirect, needs patient feedback that cannot be obtained here.
  67. The correct answer is A (Conditioned stimulus, a formerly neutral cue that now predicts the pain and triggers a learned response): The injection is the unconditioned stimulus and the crying to pain is the unconditioned response. Repeated pairing made the formerly neutral waiting room a conditioned stimulus that elicits conditioned crying. Operant concepts such as reinforcers and discriminative stimuli concern voluntary behavior shaped by consequences, not this reflexive association.
  68.
