The correct answer is E (Sibson fascia, also called the suprapleural membrane, suspending the cupola from the neck): Sibson fascia is the suprapleural membrane, which suspends the cupola from the lowest cervical transverse process and the first rib, so tension there restricts the first rib and the thoracic inlet. The prevertebral layer lies nearby but does not cap the lung apex.
  69. The correct answer is D (Carcinoembryonic antigen, a glycoprotein elevated in many bowel cancers and useful for tracking relapse after resection): Carcinoembryonic antigen is the marker followed after resection of colorectal adenocarcinoma, because a rising level after normalization suggests recurrence. It is not a screening test, but it is the appropriate surveillance marker here. The other markers track tumors from different tissues and are not expected to rise with colonic adenocarcinoma.
  70. The correct answer is C (Para-aminohippurate, which the proximal tubule extracts almost completely from blood during a single circulation): Para-aminohippurate is filtered and then secreted by organic anion transporters, so about 90 percent is cleared in one pass and its clearance approximates effective renal plasma flow. Dividing by one minus the hematocrit converts plasma flow to blood flow. Inulin measures filtration only because it is not secreted.
  71. The correct answer is A (Aldose reductase converts excess glucose to sorbitol, which accumulates and draws water into susceptible cells): In hyperglycemia the polyol pathway shunts excess glucose through aldose reductase to sorbitol. Lens and nerve cells have little sorbitol dehydrogenase, so sorbitol builds up, draws water in, and contributes to cataracts and neuropathy. Advanced glycation and protein kinase C activation also contribute to complications but do not produce a sugar alcohol.
  72. The correct answer is A (Type II, a fracture traversing the physis and metaphysis with the joint surface spared): The triangular metaphyseal fragment is the Thurston-Holland fragment of a type II injury, the most common Salter-Harris pattern. The epiphysis stays attached to the physis and the joint surface is intact, which separates it from type III and type IV injuries.
  73. The correct answer is C (Germline mutation in a DNA mismatch repair gene, causing microsatellite instability and hereditary nonpolyposis colorectal cancer): Early onset right sided colon cancer with endometrial cancer in relatives and loss of MLH1 and PMS2 in a tumor without BRAF mutation points to Lynch syndrome, a germline defect in mismatch repair that causes microsatellite instability. Sporadic MLH1 methylation also silences MLH1 but is accompanied by BRAF V600E and lacks this family pattern.
  74. The correct answer is C (Paradoxical rise in serum gastrin after intravenous secretin, reflecting autonomous release from tumor cells): Ulcers in unusual locations with diarrhea, gastric acid hypersecretion and elevated gastrin suggest Zollinger-Ellison syndrome. Gastrinoma cells respond paradoxically to secretin with a rise in gastrin, whereas normal G cells are suppressed. A high pH with high gastrin indicates achlorhydria, as in atrophic gastritis.
  75. The correct answer is E (Severe aortic regurgitation with the regurgitant jet impinging on the anterior mitral leaflet): Wide pulse pressure, bounding pulses, and an early diastolic murmur establish severe aortic regurgitation. The regurgitant jet pushes the anterior mitral leaflet and partly closes it, making a functional apical rumble called the Austin Flint murmur. True mitral stenosis would produce an opening snap and does not explain the peripheral signs.
  76. The correct answer is D (The data are compatible with no association, so the result is not statistically significant): The confidence interval includes 1.0, so the data are compatible with no association and the result is not statistically significant at the 0.05 level. This does not prove absence of association, because the interval is wide and also compatible with a 2-fold increase. The point estimate alone would not justify claiming a 40 percent increase.
  77. The correct answer is B (Stevens-Johnson syndrome, a drug reaction with mucosal erosions and under ten percent epidermal loss): Detachment under 10 percent of body surface with mucosal erosions and atypical targets after a new drug defines Stevens-Johnson syndrome, whereas detachment above 30 percent would be toxic epidermal necrolysis. Erythema multiforme major is usually herpes related with raised typical targets, and scalded skin spares the mucosa.
  78. The correct answer is B (Local progestin effect thickening cervical mucus and thinning the endometrium, blocking sperm passage and implantation): The levonorgestrel intrauterine system acts mainly locally, thickening cervical mucus to impede sperm and thinning the endometrium. Systemic levels are low enough that many users continue to ovulate, as the luteal progesterone here shows. Ovulation suppression is the dominant mechanism of combined hormonal pills, and copper release belongs to the copper device.
  79. The correct answer is A (Failure of the septum primum to fuse with the septum secundum after birth): A patent foramen ovale results when the flap of septum primum never seals against the septum secundum, leaving a slit that opens only when right atrial pressure rises, such as after Valsalva release. A venous clot can then cross to the left side and embolize to the brain. A fixed secundum defect would show a persistent shunt and usually a flow murmur.
  80. The correct answer is C (Insulin surge after carbohydrate delivery pushes phosphate intracellularly for glycolytic intermediates and ATP synthesis): In chronic starvation, total body phosphate is depleted despite a normal serum level. When carbohydrate is reintroduced, insulin stimulates cellular uptake of phosphate, potassium, and magnesium and drives phosphorylation reactions, causing the profound drop that precipitates arrhythmia and weakness. Poor absorption or purging would lower stores earlier, but the day 3 timing points to this intracellular shift.
  81. The correct answer is A (Caplan syndrome, with necrobiotic nodules developing in lungs already affected by dust-related pneumoconiosis): Caplan syndrome is the combination of rheumatoid arthritis and pneumoconiosis, here from coal dust, producing multiple well-defined peripheral necrobiotic nodules. Progressive massive fibrosis forms large conglomerate masses in the upper lobes rather than discrete peripheral nodules. Metastases and granulomatous disease are possible but are less likely with this background.
  82. The correct answer is D (Benzonatate, a local anesthetic analog that blunts the afferent limb of the cough reflex): Benzonatate anesthetizes stretch receptors in the lungs and pleura, suppressing cough without opioid or serotonergic activity. Dextromethorphan can precipitate serotonin syndrome with an SSRI, and fluoxetine inhibits its metabolism. Codeine is a poor choice after opioid use disorder, and expectorants and decongestants do not treat a dry cough.
  83. The correct answer is B (Pars interarticularis stress reaction, a precursor to a fracture of the posterior vertebral arch): Repetitive hyperextension in a young athlete causes pars interarticularis stress injury, producing back pain on extension and a positive single leg hyperextension test. Plain films are often normal early, so advanced imaging may be needed. Disc herniation worsens with flexion, and adolescent scoliosis is usually painless.
  84. The correct answer is C (T10 dermatome, which wraps around the abdomen at the level of the umbilicus): The T10 dermatome supplies the skin around the umbilicus, so loss below that line places the sensory level at T10. T4 is the nipple line, T7 the xiphoid, T12 the suprapubic area, and L1 the groin. Normal arm function and breathing fit a thoracolumbar level rather than a cervical injury.
  85. The correct answer is E (Cobalamin bound to intrinsic factor and conjugated bile acids absorbed in the distal ileum): The terminal ileum is the only site of absorption for the intrinsic factor and cobalamin complex and for reuptake of conjugated bile acids, so resection causes macrocytic anemia with neuropathy and bile acid diarrhea. Iron, folate, calcium, and sugars are absorbed in the proximal small bowel and are spared.
  86. The correct answer is E (Idiopathic interruption of femoral head blood supply followed by bone death and gradual revascularization): Legg Calve Perthes disease is idiopathic osteonecrosis of the femoral head in children aged 4 to 8, producing a painless limp, restricted abduction and rotation, and a dense flattened epiphysis on radiograph. The slipped epiphysis of adolescence and infection have different ages and findings. Synovitis does not change bone density.
  87. The correct answer is A (Prothrombin time with INR, reflecting rapid turnover of vitamin K dependent factor VII): Factor VII has a half-life of only a few hours, so the INR falls out of range quickly when synthesis fails and tracks function in real time once vitamin K is replete. Aminotransferases report hepatocyte injury, not capacity. Albumin lags by weeks and is confounded by nutrition and third spacing.
  88. The correct answer is D (Restricted range of motion on palpatory testing at the involved segment or region): TART stands for tissue texture change, asymmetry, restriction of motion, and tenderness, so the missing element is restriction of motion found on motion testing. Serum markers, osteophytes, and radicular signs may accompany back pain but are not part of the somatic dysfunction definition.
  89. The correct answer is A (Montelukast, a cysteinyl leukotriene receptor antagonist carrying a boxed warning for neuropsychiatric events): Montelukast is the once-daily leukotriene receptor antagonist used for mild persistent asthma and allergic rhinitis, and it carries a boxed warning for neuropsychiatric effects such as nightmares, irritability and depression. Zafirlukast is dosed twice daily, and zileuton is dosed more often and mainly threatens the liver. Theophylline toxicity is arrhythmic and convulsive.
  90. The correct answer is C (Basis pontis, where rapid osmotic shifts cause demyelination of descending corticospinal and corticobulbar motor fibers): Raising the sodium by more than about 8 to 10 mEq/L in 24 hours in a chronically hyponatremic, malnourished patient dehydrates brain cells and injures oligodendrocytes, classically in the basis pontis. Corticospinal and corticobulbar damage gives quadriparesis and bulbar palsy with preserved vertical eye movements, the locked-in picture. Thiamine deficiency produces a different, earlier syndrome.
  91. The correct answer is C (Donor antibodies to leukocyte antigens activating recipient neutrophils within the pulmonary capillary bed): Acute noncardiogenic pulmonary edema within six hours of a plasma-containing product, with fever, hypotension, a normal natriuretic peptide and flat neck veins, is transfusion-related acute lung injury. Donor anti-HLA or anti-neutrophil antibodies activate recipient neutrophils in the lung and increase capillary permeability. Circulatory overload would show raised venous pressure and a high natriuretic peptide.
  92. The correct answer is D (Oral isotretinoin, which shrinks sebaceous glands and reduces Cutibacterium acnes colonization and inflammation): Severe nodulocystic acne with scarring that has failed a topical retinoid plus an oral antibiotic is the classic indication for isotretinoin, the only agent that durably reduces sebum output. Spironolactone is used in women with hormonal acne and does not suit a boy, while topical or intralesional measures cannot control widespread nodules.
  93. The correct answer is B (Asymmetric atrophy of frontal and anterior temporal lobes with relative sparing of the hippocampus): Early personality change, disinhibition, hyperorality, and apathy with preserved memory describe behavioral variant frontotemporal dementia. Imaging shows frontal and anterior temporal atrophy, and the hippocampus is relatively spared early. Posterior and hippocampal atrophy characterizes Alzheimer disease.
  94. The correct answer is E (Periarteriolar lymphoid sheath, the T cell compartment that surrounds each central arteriole): The periarteriolar lymphoid sheath is the T cell zone of the splenic white pulp that surrounds the central arteriole, and it stains for T cell markers. B cells occupy the adjacent follicles, and the marginal zone lies at the outer edge of the white pulp. Cords of Billroth and sinusoids belong to the red pulp.
  95. The correct answer is E (Type I group curve, sidebent left with vertebral bodies rotated right toward the convexity): A curve bowing to the right is concave left, so the group is sidebent left, and a neutral group curve follows type I mechanics with rotation to the opposite side, which here is right toward the convexity. Type II coupling applies to single non-neutral segments rather than a multi-segment curve.
  96. The correct answer is C (Preeclampsia with HELLP syndrome from endothelial injury causing microangiopathic hemolysis and hepatic ischemia): Hypertension with proteinuria after 20 weeks defines preeclampsia, and hemolysis with elevated liver enzymes and low platelets identifies the HELLP variant. Schistocytes reflect microangiopathic hemolysis from the endothelial injury. Acute fatty liver of pregnancy tends to cause hypoglycemia and coagulopathy rather than severe hypertension, and thrombotic thrombocytopenic purpura would not improve with delivery.
  97. The correct answer is B (Engage flexion, right rotation, and right sidebending, then have the patient push toward freedom against counterforce): Muscle energy is a direct technique, so the physician engages the restrictive barrier, here flexion, right rotation, and right sidebending, and the patient pushes isometrically toward the direction of freedom against a counterforce. Holding the position of ease describes an indirect method, and a thrust belongs to a different technique.
  98. The correct answer is D (Posttraumatic stress disorder, because intrusion, avoidance and arousal symptoms persist beyond one month after the trauma): Reexperiencing, avoidance, negative mood changes and hyperarousal after a traumatic exposure make this a trauma disorder, and duration beyond one month separates posttraumatic stress disorder from acute stress disorder, which lasts three days to one month. Adjustment disorder is reserved for presentations that do not meet full criteria for another disorder.
  99. The correct answer is A (L5 root, typically compressed by a posterolateral L4 to L5 disc herniation): L5 radiculopathy produces weakness of great toe extension and ankle dorsiflexion with numbness on the dorsum of the foot and a preserved ankle reflex. A posterolateral herniation at L4 to L5 compresses the traversing L5 root. S1 radiculopathy instead weakens plantar flexion and abolishes the ankle jerk.
  100. The correct answer is A (Needle shaped crystals that appear yellow, reflecting negative birefringence of monosodium urate): Acute podagra in a patient with kidney disease and aspirin use points to gout, whose monosodium urate crystals are needle shaped and negatively birefringent, appearing yellow when parallel to the compensator axis. Calcium pyrophosphate crystals are rhomboid, weakly positively birefringent and blue when parallel, which makes them the tempting mix up.

Practice Test 3 Answer Key

  1. The correct answer is D (Autoantibodies against postsynaptic nicotinic acetylcholine receptors reducing endplate potentials at the neuromuscular junction): Fatigable ptosis and diplopia with normal pupils and reflexes, improving with rest or cold, are typical of myasthenia gravis. The antibodies target postsynaptic acetylcholine receptors, reducing receptor number and endplate potentials. Lambert-Eaton syndrome involves presynaptic calcium channels and strength that improves with repeated effort.
  2. The correct answer is E (Staphylococcus aureus, which adheres to normal valve endothelium through fibronectin binding surface proteins): Injection drug use with a tricuspid murmur that grows with inspiration and septic pulmonary emboli points to right sided endocarditis, most often from Staphylococcus aureus. Viridans streptococci infect damaged left sided valves subacutely. Enterococcus, coagulase negative staphylococci, and Streptococcus gallolyticus have different exposures.
  3. The correct answer is B (Drug X is more potent, and the two drugs are equally efficacious at the same receptor): Potency is the dose needed for a given effect and is read from the horizontal position of the curve, so the curve to the left belongs to the more potent drug. Efficacy is the maximal effect, which is identical because both curves reach the same plateau. Sedation is a separate effect and does not change either property for analgesia.
  4. The correct answer is E (Left posterior inferior cerebellar artery or its parent vertebral artery supplying the lateral medulla): Crossed sensory loss, dysphagia and hoarseness from nucleus ambiguus involvement, ipsilateral Horner syndrome, and ataxia define the lateral medullary syndrome. It results from occlusion of the posterior inferior cerebellar artery or the vertebral artery. The anterior inferior cerebellar artery lesion adds facial paralysis and hearing loss, and medial medullary infarcts cause weakness.
  5. The correct answer is B (Primary aldosteronism from autonomous adrenal mineralocorticoid production suppressing plasma renin activity): Hypertension with hypokalemia, metabolic alkalosis, suppressed renin, and a high aldosterone level indicates primary aldosteronism. Renal artery stenosis would raise renin. Liddle syndrome, apparent mineralocorticoid excess, and Cushing syndrome mimic the electrolyte picture but have low aldosterone.
  6. The correct answer is D (Dissociative amnesia with dissociative fugue, triggered by severe psychological stress with intact basic cognition): Sudden inability to recall personal information after severe stress, with purposeful travel and a normal medical workup, is dissociative amnesia with fugue. Transient global amnesia keeps identity intact and is anterograde, and Korsakoff syndrome follows chronic thiamine deficiency with confabulation. Dissociative identity disorder needs distinct personality states.
  7. The correct answer is A (Anterior to the L1 and L2 vertebral bodies, to the right of the aorta): The cisterna chyli lies anterior to L1 and L2 and gathers lymph from the lumbar and intestinal trunks before the duct ascends. The duct passes through the aortic hiatus at T12, a common error because the sac and the hiatus are close but distinct.
  8. The correct answer is E (Ornithine transcarbamylase, an X linked defect that diverts mitochondrial carbamoyl phosphate into pyrimidine synthesis): Hyperammonemia with high orotic acid and low citrulline in a male infant with an affected maternal uncle indicates ornithine transcarbamylase deficiency. Carbamoyl phosphate accumulates, leaves the mitochondrion, and enters the pyrimidine pathway, raising orotic acid. Carbamoyl phosphate synthetase I deficiency is similar but gives normal orotic acid.
  9. The correct answer is D (The area beneath the plotted line, where 1.0 is perfect and 0.5 is chance): The area under the ROC curve summarizes discrimination across all cutoffs: 1.0 means perfect separation and 0.5 means performance no better than chance. Individual points on the curve show the trade-off at one cutoff, and the far right end reflects high sensitivity with low specificity, not good overall performance.
  10. The correct answer is A (Neural reflex releasing oxytocin from the posterior pituitary to contract myoepithelial cells around alveoli): Suckling and conditioned cues like an infant's cry trigger a neural reflex that releases oxytocin from the posterior pituitary, contracting myoepithelial cells and ejecting milk. The same hormone contracts the uterus, explaining the cramps. Prolactin drives milk synthesis, not ejection, and dopamine inhibits it.
  11. The correct answer is D (Long-standing somatic dysfunction maintained by a viscerosomatic reflex from renal disease acting through sympathetic outflow): Cool, dry, ropy, fibrotic and minimally tender tissue is the chronic texture pattern, and T10 to L1 matches the sympathetic viscerosomatic level of the kidney. Years of renal disease can sustain this reflex. Warm, moist, boggy findings would indicate an acute process, and the vagus does not produce paraspinal texture change at these levels.
  12. The correct answer is D (Sensitized CD4 T cells release cytokines and recruit macrophages hours after allergen re-exposure): Induration at 48 hours without an early wheal marks a delayed type hypersensitivity response: memory CD4 T cells recognize antigen presented on macrophages and release interferon gamma and other cytokines that recruit and activate macrophages. Immediate IgE reactions peak within minutes, and immune complex reactions in the Arthus pattern peak within hours with vascular damage.
  13. The correct answer is B (Lipohyalinosis of small penetrating lenticulostriate arteries damaged by chronic hypertension and diabetes): A pure motor hemiparesis with no cortical signs and a small deep capsular infarct defines a lacunar stroke. The mechanism is lipohyalinosis of small penetrating arteries driven by hypertension and diabetes. Embolic and large artery mechanisms usually produce cortical deficits such as aphasia, neglect, or visual field loss.
  14. The correct answer is D (Superior segment of the right lower lobe, the most dependent bronchial opening in supine position): In the supine position the superior segment of the lower lobe, together with the posterior segment of the upper lobe, is the most dependent, and the right bronchus is wider and straighter, so the right superior segment is a typical site. Upright aspiration favors the basal segments, and the other choices fit different body positions.
  15. The correct answer is E (Amiloride, which blocks the epithelial sodium channel that carries lithium into collecting duct principal cells): Lithium enters principal cells through the epithelial sodium channel and impairs aquaporin 2 expression and signaling, causing nephrogenic diabetes insipidus that does not respond to desmopressin. Amiloride blocks that entry and is first-line when lithium must continue. Thiazides also reduce urine volume but raise lithium levels, which is hazardous when the level is already high-normal.
  16. The correct answer is D (Blood enters through the distal pole, so a waist fracture strands the proximal fragment): The scaphoid receives most of its blood from radial artery branches that enter near the distal pole and run retrograde, so a waist or proximal fracture can leave the proximal fragment ischemic. This is why snuffbox tenderness is treated as a fracture despite normal early films.
  17. The correct answer is A (Pyruvate dehydrogenase complex, converting pyruvate to acetyl-CoA for entry into the citric acid cycle): A block in pyruvate dehydrogenase sends pyruvate toward lactate and alanine while the cytosolic redox state, and so the lactate to pyruvate ratio, stays normal. Carbohydrate loading worsens acidosis, and the purely ketogenic amino acids lysine and leucine provide acetyl-CoA without needing the complex. Respiratory chain and pyruvate carboxylase defects would raise the ratio.
  18. The correct answer is E (Right axis deviation, reflecting right ventricular hypertrophy from chronic pressure overload): A negative QRS in lead I with a positive aVF places the axis between plus 90 and plus 180 degrees, which is right axis deviation. In pulmonary hypertension the right ventricle hypertrophies and shifts the mean vector rightward. Lateral infarction can cause right axis deviation but does not fit a chronic pulmonary vascular disease.
  19. The correct answer is E (Thoracic inlet, respiratory diaphragm, then pelvic diaphragm, inguinal region, thigh, and finally the leg): Lymph from the leg must travel through the inguinal region, pelvic diaphragm, and respiratory diaphragm before it reaches the thoracic duct terminus, so the central sites are opened first and the periphery is treated last. Starting at the foot pushes fluid into restricted channels that cannot yet accept it.
  20. The correct answer is A (Induction of hepatic CYP enzymes accelerating clearance of the anticoagulant after several weeks of exposure): Rifampin induces cytochrome P450 enzymes through nuclear receptor activation, which takes days to weeks and speeds metabolism of warfarin, lowering the INR. Displacement from albumin would raise free drug, not lower the effect. Warfarin is cleared by hepatic metabolism, so renal handling does not explain the change.
  21. The correct answer is C (Intense renal vasoconstriction caused by splanchnic arterial vasodilation and reduced effective circulating volume): Low urine sodium, a bland sediment and no response to albumin challenge fit hepatorenal syndrome, in which splanchnic vasodilation lowers effective arterial volume and drives severe renal vasoconstriction. Acute tubular necrosis would have urine sodium above 40 and muddy brown casts. Prerenal azotemia from volume loss would correct with the albumin.
  22. The correct answer is C (Dermal proliferation of spindle cells with collagen trapping at the lesion periphery and overlying epidermal hyperplasia): A firm brown papule on the leg that dimples when pinched is a dermatofibroma, a benign fibrohistiocytic proliferation in the dermis. Histology shows spindle cells entrapping collagen bundles at the edge with overlying epidermal hyperplasia. Nevus nests, horn cysts and in situ carcinoma do not produce the dimple sign.
  23. The correct answer is B (Alveolar collapse from reduced lung volumes and shallow breathing after upper abdominal surgery): Fever, bibasilar crackles, and linear basal opacities on the first postoperative day are the classic picture of atelectasis, caused by shallow breathing and reduced lung volumes after upper abdominal surgery. Pneumonia typically appears later with focal consolidation, and pulmonary embolism, edema, and aspiration have different timing and findings.
  24. The correct answer is B (Saturation of hepatic metabolism so elimination shifts from first-order toward zero-order kinetics): Phenytoin metabolism by hepatic enzymes saturates within the therapeutic range, so small dose increases cause large, nonlinear rises in concentration. Toxicity here shows as nystagmus, ataxia, and dysarthria. Enzyme induction would lower the level, and the drug is not meaningfully cleared by the kidney.
  25. The correct answer is A (Acetaminophen at limited daily doses, avoiding prostaglandin inhibition in the stomach and kidneys): Acetaminophen is the preferred first oral agent for osteoarthritis when NSAIDs are unsafe, and this woman's kidney disease, warfarin, and prior ulcer bleed all argue against an NSAID. Celecoxib spares gastric mucosa somewhat but still impairs renal prostaglandins. Taking an NSAID with food or acid suppression only partly lowers bleeding risk, and tramadol adds fall and serotonergic risks.
  26. The correct answer is A (Slipped capital femoral epiphysis, posteroinferior displacement of the femoral head through the growth plate): Referred knee pain, obligate external rotation on hip flexion and obesity at the age of 13 are classic for slipped capital femoral epiphysis, in which the epiphysis slips posteriorly and inferiorly off the neck through the weak physis. Perthes disease occurs in younger children, and normal inflammatory markers argue against septic arthritis.
  27. The correct answer is B (Sildenafil, which inhibits phosphodiesterase type 5 and raises cyclic guanosine monophosphate in vascular smooth muscle): Pulmonary arterial hypertension is defined by a high mean pressure with a normal wedge pressure. Sildenafil blocks degradation of cyclic guanosine monophosphate, amplifying nitric oxide mediated relaxation. Bosentan is also an oral therapy but acts on endothelin, and a calcium channel blocker is reserved for patients who respond to acute vasodilator testing.
  28. The correct answer is A (Endometrial glands and stroma with hemosiderin-laden macrophages lining a cavity filled with old blood): Cyclic pelvic pain, dyspareunia, infertility, uterosacral nodularity, and a ground-glass unilocular ovarian cyst describe an endometrioma. Histology shows endometrial glands and stroma with hemosiderin-laden macrophages and old blood. A corpus luteum cyst can hemorrhage but is functional and does not explain the chronic pain and infertility.
  29. The correct answer is C (Falling malonyl CoA levels relieve inhibition of carnitine acyltransferase I, increasing fatty acid entry into mitochondria): Without insulin, acetyl CoA carboxylase activity falls, malonyl CoA drops, and carnitine acyltransferase I is released from inhibition, so fatty acids enter mitochondria for beta oxidation. The resulting acetyl CoA exceeds citric acid cycle capacity and is converted to ketone bodies. Insulin deficiency raises hormone sensitive lipase activity, not lowers it.
  30. The correct answer is D (Early fall in protein C creating a transient hypercoagulable state with dermal microvascular thrombosis): Protein C has a short half-life, so its level falls before the vitamin K dependent procoagulant factors, briefly tipping the balance toward thrombosis in the skin microvasculature. This causes warfarin induced skin necrosis, usually on days three to five, especially when no bridging anticoagulant is given. Heparin induced thrombocytopenia does not fit normal platelets without heparin.
  31. The correct answer is D (Reduced NADPH generation from the pentose phosphate pathway, limiting regeneration of reduced glutathione): Glucose-6-phosphate dehydrogenase is the first enzyme of the pentose phosphate pathway and supplies NADPH, which regenerates reduced glutathione to neutralize hydrogen peroxide. Without it, oxidants denature hemoglobin into Heinz bodies, and splenic macrophages remove them as bite cells. Pyruvate kinase deficiency impairs ATP, not oxidant defense.
  32. The correct answer is C (Defer the live combined vaccine until CD4 count recovers above 200 cells per microliter): Live measles-containing vaccine is contraindicated in adults with HIV and a CD4 count below 200 because vaccine virus can disseminate. With antiretroviral therapy his count is rising, so vaccination should wait until it exceeds that threshold. A low titer does not change the contraindication, and fractional doses are not recognized.
  33. The correct answer is B (Human herpesvirus 6 infection with a blanching macular rash appearing as fever breaks in a toddler): Roseola infantum from human herpesvirus 6 gives several days of high fever in an otherwise well toddler, then a rash as the fever breaks. Parvovirus B19 produces a slapped-cheek pattern without a preceding high fever, and measles brings a prominent respiratory prodrome that this child lacked.
  34. The correct answer is D (High placental gonadotropin concentrations weakly activate the thyrotropin receptor because of shared structural homology): Human chorionic gonadotropin peaks at roughly 8 to 12 weeks and shares an alpha subunit and similar beta structure with thyrotropin, so it weakly stimulates the thyrotropin receptor and transiently suppresses thyroid stimulating hormone. This gestational transient thyrotoxicosis often accompanies hyperemesis and resolves as hCG falls. Graves disease would typically show a goiter, eye findings and persistence. Estrogen raises binding globulin and total, not free, thyroxine.
  35. The correct answer is B (Hypokalemic, hypochloremic metabolic alkalosis with elevated serum amylase from salivary gland stimulation): Repeated vomiting loses hydrogen, chloride and potassium, producing hypokalemic hypochloremic metabolic alkalosis, and parotid enlargement raises salivary amylase. Laxative abuse causes a non-anion-gap acidosis, and the starvation findings belong to low weight anorexia, not a normal weight patient with binge and purge behavior.
  36. The correct answer is B (Impaired cerebrospinal fluid absorption at arachnoid granulations producing communicating hydrocephalus with normal pressure): The triad of gait disturbance, urinary urgency, and cognitive slowing with ventriculomegaly that improves after lumbar puncture indicates normal pressure hydrocephalus. The proposed mechanism is reduced absorption of cerebrospinal fluid over the convexities. In ex vacuo enlargement sulci widen, and drainage would not improve gait.
  37. The correct answer is E (Lead-time bias, because diagnosis moves earlier without delaying the actual time of death): When screening finds disease earlier but does not change the date of death, survival measured from diagnosis looks longer even though patients live no longer. That is lead-time bias. Length-time bias would instead arise from slow-growing tumors being overrepresented among screen-detected cases.
  38. The correct answer is D (Place a urethral catheter to relieve the outlet obstruction and monitor closely for postobstructive diuresis): A large residual bladder volume with bilateral hydronephrosis in a man with prostatic symptoms points to a postrenal cause, and decompression with a bladder catheter usually restores function. Urine output may rise sharply afterward, so fluids and electrolytes need monitoring. Fluids, diuretics, and biopsy do not address the blockage.
  39. The correct answer is D (Translocation between chromosomes 11 and 22 creating an EWSR1 and FLI1 fusion transcription factor): Small round blue cells with PAS-positive glycogen, onion-skin periosteal reaction and a diaphyseal lesion in an adolescent with systemic symptoms indicate Ewing sarcoma, characterized by t(11;22) producing the EWSR1 and FLI1 fusion. Osteosarcoma more often arises in the metaphysis and carries RB1 and TP53 loss, which is the tempting alternative.
  40. The correct answer is C (A single intramuscular dose of benzathine penicillin G, given after confirming he has no allergy): A painless indurated ulcer with motile spirochetes on darkfield is primary syphilis, which is treated with a single intramuscular dose of benzathine penicillin G in patients without allergy. Herpes and chancroid cause painful ulcers, and the gonorrhea regimen does not cover Treponema pallidum.
  41. The correct answer is B (Posterior and inferior displacement of the femoral epiphysis through the physeal hypertrophic zone): Obesity, adolescent age, knee pain, and obligate external rotation on hip flexion indicate slipped capital femoral epiphysis, where the epiphysis displaces posteriorly and inferiorly through the hypertrophic zone of the physis. Knee pain is referred through the obturator nerve, so hip examination is essential. Normal markers argue against infection.
  42. The correct answer is E (Noninvasive positive pressure ventilation by tight face mask, with controlled low-flow oxygen titrated to saturation): Acute hypercapnic respiratory acidosis with a pH below 7.35 in an alert patient with COPD is an indication for noninvasive positive pressure ventilation, which lowers intubation rates and mortality. Oxygen should be titrated to a saturation of about 88 to 92 percent to avoid worsening carbon dioxide retention. Intubation is reserved for those who fail it or cannot protect the airway.
  43. The correct answer is E (Human chorionic somatomammotropin, which antagonizes insulin and promotes lipolysis to spare glucose for the fetus): Human placental lactogen, also called chorionic somatomammotropin, rises in proportion to placental mass and is a major diabetogenic placental hormone, antagonizing insulin in maternal tissues so more glucose reaches the fetus. Progesterone, cortisol and other hormones contribute modestly. Relaxin acts mainly on ligaments and does not drive glucose intolerance.
  44. The correct answer is A (Lower in the screening population because most positive results there come from false positives): Predictive values depend on prevalence. At 1 percent prevalence with 95 percent specificity, false positives from the large healthy group outnumber true positives, giving a positive predictive value near 16 percent, whereas at 40 percent prevalence it exceeds 90 percent. Sensitivity and specificity stay fixed, but they do not alone determine predictive value.
  45. The correct answer is A (Lower leptin from reduced fat mass and higher ghrelin from the stomach, both increasing hunger): Weight loss reduces adipose mass, so circulating leptin falls, removing a satiety signal to the hypothalamus, while ghrelin rises, promoting hunger. This adaptive response, together with lower energy expenditure, promotes weight regain. Peptide YY and cholecystokinin are satiety signals that tend to fall or stay unchanged, not hunger signals.
  46. The correct answer is D (Perihepatitis with inflammation of the liver capsule from ascending pelvic infection, producing adhesions): Pleuritic right upper quadrant pain after pelvic inflammatory disease with normal liver tests and a normal gallbladder is Fitz-Hugh-Curtis syndrome, an infection of the liver capsule that leaves violin-string adhesions. A subphrenic abscess would usually bring ongoing fever and a visible collection. The negative Murphy sign argues against cholecystitis.
  47. The correct answer is A (Salmonella species that translocate from the gut and seed poorly perfused infarcted bone): Sickle cell disease causes bone infarcts and gut barrier changes, so Salmonella species are disproportionately recovered from osteomyelitis in these patients. Staphylococcus aureus remains the most common single cause overall and must still be covered empirically, which is why it is tempting here. Pseudomonas follows puncture wounds and Pasteurella follows animal bites.
  48. The correct answer is A (Progesterone relaxing lower esophageal sphincter smooth muscle, lowering barrier pressure during gestation): Rising progesterone relaxes smooth muscle, including the lower esophageal sphincter, and the enlarging uterus adds intra-abdominal pressure, so reflux is common in late pregnancy. Gastric acid secretion actually falls in pregnancy, so an acid hypersecretion mechanism does not explain the symptoms.
  49. The correct answer is D (Binds hydroxyapatite and induces osteoclast apoptosis by inhibiting farnesyl pyrophosphate synthase in the mevalonate pathway): The fasting, upright, full glass of water instructions describe an oral nitrogen containing bisphosphonate such as alendronate. It concentrates in bone at sites of remodeling and inhibits farnesyl pyrophosphate synthase, which triggers osteoclast apoptosis and reduces fracture risk. RANKL antibodies and teriparatide are injected, and raloxifene and calcitonin are not first-line choices.
  50. The correct answer is E (Pericardial drainage to relieve impaired diastolic filling from fluid surrounding the heart): Hypotension, distended neck veins, and muffled heart sounds after penetrating chest trauma form Beck triad, indicating cardiac tamponade, an obstructive shock. Removing the pericardial fluid restores ventricular filling. Normal, equal breath sounds and a midline trachea argue against tension pneumothorax or hemothorax, and volume or inotropes do not relieve compression.
  51. The correct answer is D (Order a skeletal survey and report suspected abuse to child protective services today): A spiral long bone fracture in a nonambulatory infant with a mechanism that does not fit is a red flag for inflicted injury. Physicians must report reasonable suspicion, not proof, and a skeletal survey looks for other occult fractures. Metabolic or genetic workups can follow but must not delay protection of the child.
  52. The correct answer is C (Restoring glutathione stores so the toxic reactive metabolite is conjugated and detoxified before binding hepatocyte proteins): At toxic doses the sulfation and glucuronidation pathways saturate and more drug is oxidized by CYP2E1 to NAPQI, which depletes glutathione. N-acetylcysteine replenishes glutathione so NAPQI is conjugated. Activated charcoal limits absorption but does not neutralize metabolite already formed.
  53. The correct answer is C (B cell lymphoma, most often marginal zone type arising within the salivary glands): Sjogren syndrome carries a markedly increased risk of non-Hodgkin B cell lymphoma, particularly marginal zone lymphoma of mucosa associated tissue, driven by chronic B cell stimulation. Interstitial lung disease can occur but is not the greatest relative risk. Hodgkin lymphoma and oral squamous carcinoma are not the characteristic malignant associations.
  54. The correct answer is E (Rupture of a vasa recta artery stretched across the neck of a diverticulum): Diverticula form where the vasa recta penetrate the circular muscle, so the artery runs over the dome or neck and can rupture, causing painless brisk bleeding. Angiodysplasia is a cecal lesion without diverticula, and ischemic colitis and varices have distinct histories absent here.
  55. The correct answer is C (Oblique axis, with sacral torsion occurring as the lumbar spine rotates during ambulation): The sacrum twists about oblique axes during walking, producing torsion as the lumbar spine rotates. The superior transverse axis is associated with respiratory motion and the middle transverse axis with postural flexion and extension, so neither describes a diagonal gait axis.
  56. The correct answer is C (Blunted erythropoietin response as oxygen sensing shifts from liver to kidney, plus phlebotomy losses): Preterm infants have an exaggerated physiologic nadir because erythropoietin production is low as the oxygen sensing site moves from the liver to the kidney, and repeated blood draws add to the loss. A normal MCV, low reticulocyte count, and normal bilirubin argue against iron deficiency, hemolysis, and B12 deficiency.
  57. The correct answer is B (Severe discomfort with passive stretch of the muscles in the affected compartment): Pain out of proportion to the injury, made worse by passive stretching of the involved muscles, is the earliest reliable clinical sign. Pulses and capillary refill usually stay normal because compartment pressure rises above venous and capillary pressure long before it exceeds arterial pressure. Pallor and paralysis are late findings that mean irreversible injury is under way.
  58. The correct answer is A (Absent surfactant leaves tension fixed, so tiny alveoli generate greater collapsing pressure per Laplace's law): Collapsing pressure in a sphere equals twice the surface tension divided by the radius. Surfactant normally lowers tension more as alveoli shrink, which stabilizes them, but this preterm infant lacks it, so small alveoli empty into larger neighbors. Poiseuille's law describes airway resistance and does not explain this alveolar instability.
  59. The correct answer is D (Acute cellular rejection from recipient T cells reacting against donor MHC molecules on tubular epithelium): A rapid rise in creatinine months after transplant with a lymphocytic interstitial infiltrate and tubulitis, but negative C4d, defines acute cellular rejection driven by recipient T cells. A therapeutic tacrolimus level and the inflammatory infiltrate argue against drug toxicity, which shows vacuolization without infiltrate. Hyperacute rejection occurs within minutes to hours and chronic rejection shows fibrosis.
  60. The correct answer is D (Sertoli cells supporting germ cells and restraining pituitary gonadotropin secretion through a peptide hormone): Sertoli cells nurture developing germ cells and secrete the peptide hormone that inhibits pituitary FSH. Damage to the seminiferous epithelium lowers that peptide, so FSH rises while Leydig cell testosterone and LH stay normal. Germ cell loss itself does not provide the feedback signal.
  61. The correct answer is A (Admit for serial respiratory monitoring and begin intravenous immunoglobulin or plasma exchange): Ascending symmetric weakness with areflexia after a diarrheal illness, likely from Campylobacter molecular mimicry, indicates Guillain-Barre syndrome. A declining vital capacity signals risk of respiratory failure, so monitoring and immunotherapy are required. Steroids give no benefit, and cord compression would usually cause bladder dysfunction and a sensory level.
  62. The correct answer is C (Inappropriately low hepcidin from HFE dysfunction, increasing ferroportin mediated intestinal iron absorption and macrophage release): Blue granular hepatocyte iron on Prussian blue with high transferrin saturation fits hereditary hemochromatosis, where HFE dysfunction lowers hepcidin, so ferroportin stays active and the gut absorbs too much iron. High hepcidin in inflammation does the reverse and traps iron in macrophages. The history excludes transfusional overload.
  63. Induction is amphotericin B plus flucytosine, and elevated pressure is managed with repeated lumbar puncture. Fluconazole alone is used later for consolidation or maintenance, not for induction of severe disease.
  64. The correct answer is B (Visceral innervation of the mucosa above the pectinate line lacks somatic pain fibers): Internal hemorrhoids arise above the dentate line, where mucosa is supplied by visceral afferents that sense stretch but not sharp pain. External hemorrhoids and fissures lie below it and are supplied by the somatic inferior rectal nerve, which is why they hurt. Bleeding is bright red because it comes from arteriovenous cushions.
  65. The correct answer is B (Trophozoites coating the duodenal brush border, blunting villi and reducing absorptive surface area): Giardia trophozoites adhere to the duodenal and jejunal brush border without invading, causing villous blunting, disaccharidase loss, and fat malabsorption that produce greasy, foul stools. Bacterial overgrowth can also impair fat absorption, but the positive stool antigen after stream water exposure points to the protozoan. Celiac disease gives a similar biopsy but does not follow a two-week exposure history.
  66. The correct answer is E (Efferent arteriolar dilation that lowers glomerular capillary pressure when inflow through narrowed renal arteries drops): Angiotensin II constricts the efferent arteriole and so maintains glomerular capillary pressure when renal perfusion is low. Blocking it in bilateral renal artery stenosis drops filtration and raises creatinine and potassium. Prostaglandin loss from NSAIDs produces a similar picture but acts on the afferent arteriole instead.
  67. The correct answer is B (Reduced intracellular cholesterol synthesis triggers SREBP-mediated increase in surface LDL receptors that clear circulating particles): Atorvastatin inhibits HMG-CoA reductase, lowering hepatocyte cholesterol, which activates SREBP-2 and raises LDL receptor expression so more LDL is cleared from plasma. NPC1L1 blockade is ezetimibe, PPAR-alpha activation is fibrates, and increased bile acid loss is bile acid resins. Those mechanisms are real but do not account for a statin response.
  68. The correct answer is D (T3a, because thickness exceeds 2.0 mm but not 4.0 mm and ulceration is absent): T category for melanoma depends on Breslow thickness: up to 1.0 mm is T1, over 1.0 to 2.0 mm is T2, over 2.0 to 4.0 mm is T3, and over 4.0 mm is T4. The letter a or b reflects absence or presence of ulceration. A thickness of 2.4 mm without ulceration is T3a, and the negative sentinel node does not alter T category.
  69. The correct answer is C (Midgut visceral afferents traveling with sympathetic nerves and entering the cord near T10): The appendix is a midgut derivative, so early inflammation stimulates visceral afferents that accompany sympathetic fibers to the T10 segment, perceived as poorly localized pain at that dermatome around the navel. When inflammation reaches the parietal peritoneum, somatic nerves localize the pain to the right lower quadrant. Foregut and hindgut afferents enter at different levels and map to the epigastrium and suprapubic region.
  70. The correct answer is E (Calcium binding troponin C, which draws tropomyosin off the thin filament binding sites): In skeletal muscle, released calcium binds troponin C, which pulls tropomyosin off the binding sites on the thin filament so the myosin head can attach. Calmodulin and myosin light chain kinase operate in smooth muscle. ATP binding releases a bound head and ATP hydrolysis re-cocks it, so neither is the event that permits attachment.
  71. The correct answer is C (Start systemic corticosteroids to suppress the neutrophil driven inflammatory process within the ulcer): A painful ulcer with a violaceous undermined border, pathergy and negative cultures in a patient with inflammatory bowel disease is pyoderma gangrenosum, a sterile neutrophilic dermatosis. Immunosuppression is the treatment, whereas debridement provokes new lesions at the injury site.
  72. Wedge hypergranulosis with a band-like infiltrate indicates lichen planus, and spongiosis suggests eczema.
  73. The correct answer is A (The probability of a difference at least this large if the true effect were zero): A p value is the probability of observing a difference at least as large as the one found if the null hypothesis (no true effect) were correct. It is not the probability that the null is true or that chance caused the result, and it does not describe replication. Failing to detect a real effect is a type II error, a different quantity.
  74. The correct answer is D (Cervical thrust technique with rotation and extension, which stresses the vertebral arteries near the atlas): Symptoms with head rotation point to vertebrobasilar insufficiency, an absolute contraindication to cervical HVLA because rotation and extension stress the vertebral arteries. Gentle indirect and low force methods can be used while the imaging is pending.
  75. The correct answer is D (Scheduled therapeutic phlebotomy, mobilizing stored iron to make new red cells until stores normalize): Bronze skin, diabetes, arthropathy, high ferritin and transferrin saturation with HFE C282Y homozygosity indicate hereditary hemochromatosis. Because hemoglobin is normal, phlebotomy is the safest, cheapest and most effective way to deplete iron, as the marrow uses stored iron to rebuild red cells. Chelators are reserved for patients who cannot tolerate phlebotomy or have anemia.
  76. The correct answer is C (ABO incompatibility with maternal anti-A antibodies causing early extravascular hemolysis of infant red cells): Jaundice in the first 24 hours is pathologic, and the positive direct antiglobulin test, reticulocytosis, low hemoglobin, and spherocytes with an O mother and an A infant show immune hemolysis from ABO incompatibility. The unconjugated pattern also excludes biliary atresia, and the enzyme deficiency would not cause hemolysis.
  77. The correct answer is A (Bacterial fermentation acidifies colonic contents, converting ammonia to poorly absorbed ammonium that is trapped and excreted): Lactulose is a nonabsorbable disaccharide that colonic bacteria ferment to organic acids. The lower luminal pH converts diffusible ammonia to ammonium, which is trapped and eliminated, and the laxative effect speeds excretion. Rifaximin reduces urease producing bacteria, but that is a different drug mechanism.
  78. The correct answer is C (Histoplasma capsulatum, a dimorphic fungus acquired from soil enriched with bird or bat droppings): Small yeast inside macrophages in a patient with advanced HIV who has exposure to bird droppings in the Ohio and Mississippi river valleys is disseminated histoplasmosis. Blastomyces shares the region but shows broad-based budding yeast, and Cryptococcus is extracellular with a capsule. Coccidioides forms spherules instead of yeast in tissue.
  79. The correct answer is B (Sustained high estradiol from the mature follicle exerts positive feedback on the hypothalamus and pituitary): Estradiol above a threshold concentration for about two days switches from negative to positive feedback, causing a GnRH driven luteinizing hormone surge that triggers ovulation roughly 36 hours later. Progesterone from a corpus luteum is not yet present and suppresses gonadotropins after ovulation. Inhibin B and follicle stimulating hormone changes modulate follicle selection but do not generate the surge.
  80. The correct answer is C (Periodic full-body skin examinations with biopsy of any lesion that changes or looks atypical): Numerous atypical nevi with a family history of melanoma place him at high risk, and the standard approach is lifelong surveillance with total-body examination and biopsy of changing or suspicious lesions. Prophylactic excision of all nevi is impractical and does not eliminate risk since most melanomas arise de novo. Destructive treatment would prevent histologic assessment.
  81. The correct answer is C (400 mg, calculated from the total volume of distribution times the target concentration): The loading dose equals the volume of distribution times the desired concentration. The volume is 0.5 L/kg times 80 kg, or 40 L, and multiplying by 10 mg/L gives 400 mg. Half-life determines the maintenance schedule and does not enter the loading dose.
  82. The correct answer is B (Release of hypoxic vasoconstriction increasing dead space, plus Haldane displacement of CO2 from hemoglobin): Oxygen relieves hypoxic pulmonary vasoconstriction in poorly ventilated alveoli, so perfusion shifts toward units with high dead space, and oxygenated hemoglobin binds less CO2 (the Haldane effect). Loss of hypoxic drive contributes, but the change in minute ventilation is usually modest. Bicarbonate retention occurs over days, not minutes, and does not explain this rapid change.
  83. The correct answer is B (Counterstrain, an indirect passive method that moves tissue toward its position of ease and holds it): Positioning a tender point toward ease and holding about 90 seconds without force describes counterstrain. Muscle energy needs a patient contraction and HVLA needs a thrust, and both are direct methods.
  84. The correct answer is A (Ligamentum flavum, composed of elastin and spanning neighboring vertebral arches within the canal): The ligamentum flavum is rich in elastin and joins the laminae of adjacent vertebrae, so it gives the characteristic loss of resistance just before the epidural space. The longitudinal ligaments run along the vertebral bodies and are not crossed by a posterior midline needle.
  85. The correct answer is C (Combine a long-acting beta agonist with the inhaled corticosteroid in a single maintenance inhaler): Persistent symptoms on a low-dose inhaled corticosteroid, after adherence, technique and triggers are checked, call for stepping up with an inhaled corticosteroid and long-acting beta agonist combination. A long-acting beta agonist must not be used without the corticosteroid because of increased risk of severe exacerbations. Theophylline and scheduled short-acting agents are not preferred add-ons.
  86. The correct answer is D (Disconnect the ventilator briefly and then lower the respiratory rate to lengthen expiratory time): Failure of expiratory flow to reach zero indicates auto-PEEP from dynamic hyperinflation, which raises intrathoracic pressure, reduces venous return and causes hypotension. Briefly disconnecting the circuit lets trapped gas escape, and a lower rate with a longer expiratory time prevents recurrence. A midline trachea and the flow tracing argue against tension pneumothorax.
  87. The correct answer is E (Rectus femoris shortening, since this two-joint hip flexor is slackened once the knee is straightened): In the modified Thomas test, a thigh that rises with the knee flexed but drops when the knee is extended implicates the rectus femoris, because knee extension slackens that two-joint muscle. A tight iliopsoas would keep the thigh raised whatever the knee position. Abduction during the test points to the tensor fasciae latae and iliotibial band.
  88. The correct answer is A (Point mutation in factor V that makes it resistant to inactivation by activated protein C): Factor V Leiden is the most common inherited thrombophilia, and a family history of unprovoked venous clots with normal antithrombin, protein C, and protein S fits it. The altered factor V cannot be cleaved by activated protein C, so clotting continues unchecked. The prothrombin gene variant is less common, and the deficiencies were excluded by testing.
  89. The correct answer is C (Begin high dose glucocorticoids at once and pursue biopsy confirmation of the artery afterward): Jaw claudication, visual symptoms, and a very high sedimentation rate indicate giant cell arteritis, where amaurosis signals impending permanent blindness. High dose glucocorticoids should start immediately because biopsy remains positive for days after treatment begins. Low dose steroids are insufficient, and eye drops or antiplatelet therapy do not treat the arteritis.
  90. The correct answer is C (Atrioventricular node, perfused by a branch arising where the posterior descending artery originates): In right-dominant circulation, the right coronary artery gives off the posterior descending artery and the AV nodal artery at the crux of the heart. Inferior infarction from right coronary occlusion therefore commonly produces AV block. Septal perforators from the left anterior descending mainly supply the bundle branches, so they do not explain an inferior infarct.
  91. The correct answer is E (Left facial nerve carrying the efferent limb to orbicularis oculi): The corneal reflex has an afferent limb in the ophthalmic division of the trigeminal nerve and an efferent limb in the facial nerve to orbicularis oculi. Because sensation is felt on both sides and the right eye blinks to either stimulus, the afferent and central pathways work and only the left efferent limb fails. Oculomotor damage would cause ptosis rather than failure to close.
  92. The correct answer is E (Somatic dysfunction, impaired function of body framework elements and their associated neurovascular structures): Asymmetry, restricted motion, tenderness, and tissue texture change at one segment are the diagnostic criteria for somatic dysfunction, here acute given the warm, boggy tissue. Radiculopathy would cause neurologic findings, and fibromyalgia is widespread and not segmental.
  93. The correct answer is C (Osteoid osteoma, a benign osteoblastic tumor whose small nidus produces prostaglandins that drive nocturnal pain): Night pain relieved quickly by anti-inflammatory drugs with a small lucent nidus surrounded by reactive sclerosis is classic for osteoid osteoma. Osteoblastoma shares the histology but is larger than 2 cm and responds poorly to NSAIDs. A Brodie abscess can look similar but would be expected to cause infectious features and a metaphyseal location.
  94. The correct answer is E (Verapamil, a calcium channel blocker used as first-line preventive therapy for this disorder): Unilateral periorbital pain with ipsilateral tearing, rhinorrhea, ptosis, and restlessness in a circadian pattern is cluster headache. Verapamil is the preferred preventive, and oxygen or triptans treat acute attacks. Migraine preventives such as propranolol and topiramate are not first-line for this disorder.
  95. The correct answer is B (Giant cell tumor of bone, whose neoplastic stromal cells recruit osteoclast precursors via RANKL): An eccentric lytic epiphyseal lesion in a skeletally mature young adult with multinucleated cells among mononuclear stromal cells is a giant cell tumor, whose neoplastic stromal cells express RANKL. Chondroblastoma also involves the epiphysis but occurs in adolescents with open growth plates and shows chondroid matrix and grooved nuclei. Brown tumor needs high serum calcium and parathyroid hormone.
  96. The correct answer is B (Counterstrain, passively holding the region in its position of ease for about ninety seconds): Counterstrain is an indirect, passive technique that moves the tissues toward ease and avoids loading the fractured vertebra. High velocity thrust is contraindicated at a fracture, and strong leverage, springing, or maximal contraction add risk in osteoporotic bone. Gentle indirect treatment also suits her anxiety about forceful methods.
  97. The correct answer is E (Peroxidase-mediated iodide oxidation and iodination of tyrosyl residues on thyroglobulin within the colloid): Thionamides inhibit thyroid peroxidase, which oxidizes iodide and attaches it to tyrosine residues, blocking organification and coupling. Methimazole does not block peripheral T4 to T3 conversion, unlike propylthiouracil. Iodide uptake itself is the target of perchlorate.
  98. The correct answer is E (Serum ferritin and transferrin saturation to evaluate for systemic iron overload disease): Rhomboid, weakly positively birefringent crystals with chondrocalcinosis indicate calcium pyrophosphate deposition, and a patient under 60 should be screened for an underlying cause. Bronze skin, fatigue and second and third metacarpophalangeal arthropathy point to hemochromatosis, so ferritin and transferrin saturation come first. Hyperparathyroidism also predisposes but lacks these distinguishing features.
  99. The correct answer is B (Lung, with a corresponding posterior point near the transverse processes of T3 and T4): The anterior Chapman point for the lung lies in the third and fourth intercostal spaces near the sternum, with a posterior counterpart near the transverse processes of T3 and T4. The bronchial point sits a space higher, and the heart, stomach, and liver and gallbladder points correspond to other locations and levels.
  100. The correct answer is C (Linoleic acid deficiency limiting arachidonic acid synthesis needed for membrane integrity and eicosanoid production): Essential fatty acid deficiency develops within weeks when lipid is omitted from parenteral nutrition, because humans cannot synthesize linoleic acid, the precursor of arachidonic acid. It causes scaly dermatitis, alopecia, poor healing, and thrombocytopenia. Biotin and niacin deficiencies produce different skin and neurologic syndromes, and zinc deficiency gives a periorificial rash with a low zinc level.

Practice Test 4 Answer Key

  1. The correct answer is D (Netlike intercellular IgG and C3 deposition around keratinocytes throughout the lower epidermis): Pemphigus vulgaris causes acantholysis just above the basal layer, and IgG against desmoglein 3 produces a netlike chicken-wire pattern around keratinocytes. Linear basement membrane staining belongs to pemphigoid, where the blister forms below the epidermis.
  2. The correct answer is E (Acute hepatitis B in the window period, after surface antigen clearance but before antibody appears): During the window period, surface antigen has cleared and surface antibody has not yet risen, so only IgM core antibody marks acute hepatitis B. Chronic infection would keep surface antigen positive beyond six months. Resolved infection would show surface antibody and IgG core antibody.
  3. The correct answer is D (Endodermal sinus tumor arising from primitive yolk sac elements in the developing testis tissue): Yolk sac tumor is the most common testicular tumor in young boys and produces alpha-fetoprotein. Its Schiller-Duval bodies resemble glomeruli. Seminoma occurs in adults and does not make alpha-fetoprotein, and choriocarcinoma would raise beta-hCG.
  4. The correct answer is C (Central dopamine D2 receptor blockade in the nigrostriatal pathway with later receptor upregulation): Metoclopramide blocks D2 receptors and crosses the blood brain barrier, so prolonged use can cause tardive dyskinesia through nigrostriatal D2 blockade and upregulation. Its prokinetic effect comes partly from 5-HT4 agonism and cholinergic enhancement, which do not explain these movements. Long-term use in the elderly and in diabetic patients needs close monitoring.
  5. The correct answer is A (Decussating second-order spinothalamic axons in the anterior white commissure at cervical levels): A syrinx expands around the central canal and interrupts second-order pain and temperature axons as they cross in the anterior white commissure, producing a bilateral cape-like dissociated sensory loss. Dorsal columns carry touch, vibration and position sense and lie posteriorly, so they are spared. Spinothalamic tract lesions after crossing cause contralateral loss below the level instead.
  6. The correct answer is D (Secondary prevention, which detects disease early in its preclinical stage to limit progression): Screening asymptomatic adults finds disease in a latent or preclinical stage, which is the definition of secondary prevention. Primary prevention would aim to prevent hypertension from developing, and tertiary prevention would manage complications in people already diagnosed.
  7. The correct answer is C (Prospective cohort study, in which exposure groups are observed forward in time for new disease): The investigators identified hikers by repellent use and followed them forward to count new lesions, which defines a prospective cohort study. Because hikers chose repellent themselves, it is not a randomized trial. A case-control design would start from affected hikers, and a case series lacks a comparison group.
  8. The correct answer is D (Discontinue the antidepressant and begin a mood stabilizer such as lithium or valproate): Manic symptoms after starting an antidepressant, with a prior period of elevated energy, indicate bipolar disorder with antidepressant-associated mania. The antidepressant should be stopped and a mood stabilizer started, whereas more serotonergic or stimulant drugs would worsen the episode.
  9. The correct answer is A (Transverse band of the cruciate ligament, restraining forward translation of C1 on C2 in flexion): Pannus erodes the transverse band of the cruciate ligament, which normally holds the dens against the anterior arch and restrains forward translation of C1 on C2, producing atlantoaxial subluxation, seen as a widened atlantodental interval in flexion. Alar ligaments limit rotation and sidebending of the skull, so their failure would not widen this gap in flexion. Cord compression is the danger, so flexion films are obtained before intubation.

Leg predominant contralateral deficits with abulia and incontinence from paracentral and medial frontal involvement localize here. Middle cerebral infarcts affect the face and arm more than the leg and add aphasia when the dominant hemisphere is involved.

This is Brown-Sequard syndrome; bilateral patterns would suggest central or anterior cord or posterior column disease.

The correct answer is C (Myophosphorylase, which normally liberates glucose-1-phosphate from stored polysaccharide to supply glycolysis during intense exercise): Without myophosphorylase, muscle cannot mobilize glycogen, so intense exercise causes cramps and myoglobinuria with glycogen buildup, and lactate stays flat because glycolysis is not fed. The normal ammonia rise proves effort was adequate. Tarui disease can look similar but adds hemolysis, and mitochondrial disease would raise lactate early.
  13. The correct answer is E (Multiple comparisons inflate the overall type I error, so one nominal hit may be chance): Testing 20 outcomes at 0.05 each gives roughly a 64 percent chance of at least one false positive, so a single p value of 0.04 among many tests is weak evidence. A p value is not the probability that a result is false, and a failed primary endpoint does not make secondary findings invalid, only exploratory. Publication bias and type II error do not explain a single significant finding.

The ductus venosus becomes the ligamentum venosum in the liver, a tempting remnant that is anatomically distant from the aortic arch.

The correct answer is A (Arrange a more specific confirmatory assay, since most positives at this prevalence are false): At a prevalence of 0.1 percent, even a sensitive assay with moderate specificity produces mostly false positives, so a single positive result has low positive predictive value. A more specific confirmatory assay separates true from false positives. Treating immediately ignores base rates, and repeating the same assay can reproduce the same cross-reacting false positive.
  21.   22. Stratification or multivariable adjustment controls it in the analysis. A larger sample reduces random error but leaves confounding intact, and relative risk cannot be obtained directly from a case-control design.
  23. The correct answer is D (Assay X is more likely to rank a diseased patient above a nondiseased patient): The area under the ROC curve equals the probability that a randomly chosen diseased patient receives a higher result than a randomly chosen nondiseased patient, so 0.95 outperforms 0.70. It does not give sensitivity, specificity or predictive value at any single cutoff. Predictive value also depends on prevalence, which a ROC curve does not contain.
  24. Correlation in a volunteer sample cannot establish that exercise lowers heart rate, since fitter people may simply choose to exercise more. The coefficient is not the regression slope.
  25. The correct answer is A (Chi-square test of independence, which evaluates counts arranged in a two by two table): Both exposure (daycare versus home) and outcome (otitis media present or absent) are categorical, so counts in a two by two table are compared with a chi-square test. The t tests and analysis of variance need a continuous outcome summarized as a mean. A Fisher exact test would replace chi-square only if expected cell counts were small.

Counterstrain and functional technique move toward ease rather than the barrier. High velocity low amplitude uses a thrust rather than a sustained contraction.

The correct answer is E (Hypoxemia is fully explained by alveolar hypoventilation with a normal alveolar to arterial gradient): Alveolar PO2 is about 150 minus 80 divided by 0.8, which is 50 mmHg, so the alveolar to arterial gradient is near zero. A normal gradient with high PaCO2 means the hypoxemia comes from hypoventilation, and it will correct with ventilation and oxygen. Shunt, V/Q mismatch and diffusion defects all widen the gradient.

  35. The pericardial knock and calcification confirm constriction. Effusion and myocardial restriction are different structural problems.

Self-regulation and structure-function are separate tenets, rational treatment applies them to care, and population screening is a public health principle rather than a tenet.

Type I is typically found in the thoracic and lumbar spine and does not change with flexion or extension. Type II involves one non-neutral segment with sidebending and rotation to the same side.

Pain and adrenergic symptoms are treated supportively, and the course usually resolves over weeks to months.

  55. The correct answer is E (Odds exceed risks when events are frequent, so the ratio of odds overstates the risk ratio): When an outcome is frequent, the odds are noticeably larger than the risk, so the odds ratio moves further from 1 than the risk ratio and overstates it. A journalist reading 3.0 as tripled risk therefore exaggerates the effect. Odds ratios can be computed in any design, and the direction of distortion is overstatement, not understatement.

  63. The correct answer is E (Alkalemia increases albumin binding of calcium, lowering ionized calcium and raising neuromuscular excitability): Acute respiratory alkalosis from hyperventilation makes albumin more negatively charged, so more calcium binds albumin and the ionized fraction falls, even though total calcium is normal.   64. The correct answer is A (Point mutation making factor V resistant to inactivation by activated protein C): Factor V Leiden, a substitution that makes factor Va resistant to cleavage by activated protein C, is the most common inherited thrombophilia, found in a notable fraction of people of European descent. It typically presents with venous thrombosis, often with a trigger such as estrogen. Prothrombin G20210A is next most common, while the natural anticoagulant deficiencies are much rarer.
  65.   87. The correct answer is B (Preserved PaO2, lowered total oxygen content, and a leftward shift with impaired unloading): Carbon monoxide binds hemoglobin with roughly 200 times the affinity of oxygen, lowering the fraction available to carry oxygen without changing dissolved oxygen, so PaO2 stays normal. Remaining binding sites hold oxygen more tightly, shifting the curve left and impairing tissue unloading. Standard pulse oximetry reads carboxyhemoglobin as oxyhemoglobin and is falsely reassuring.
  88. The correct answer is D (Pedal pump using rhythmic passive dorsiflexion and plantarflexion of both feet with the patient supine): Repeated passive movement of the feet could dislodge a clot from a suspected deep vein thrombosis, so the pedal pump is held until the thrombosis is excluded. The thoracic, abdominal, inlet, and occipital techniques do not manipulate the leg veins and can still be used to support lymph flow.
  89. The correct answer is A (Ulnar nerve compressed behind the medial epicondyle, proximal to the dorsal cutaneous branch origin): Elbow flexion provoking symptoms, ulnar hand weakness and numbness of the dorsal ulnar hand place the lesion at the cubital tunnel, because the dorsal cutaneous branch leaves about 5 cm above the wrist. Guyon canal compression would spare the dorsal hand, and a root or lower trunk lesion would reduce medial forearm sensation.
  90. The correct answer is A (Cisterna chyli, the dilated lymphatic sac that begins the thoracic duct): The cisterna chyli lies near L1 to L2 behind the right crus and collects abdominal lymph before the thoracic duct ascends through the aortic hiatus. Restricted crural motion reduces the pumping action that helps lymph move upward. The azygos systems and vena cava are veins, and the right lymphatic duct drains only the upper right body.
  91. The correct answer is E (Gentle pedal pump using slow passive plantar flexion and dorsiflexion of both ankles): Techniques that compress the abdomen carry a risk of rupturing a pulsatile aneurysm, so they are avoided here. A pedal pump works at the ankles, away from the aorta, and still moves venous and lymphatic fluid toward the thoracic inlet.
  92. The correct answer is B (Prostaglandin D2 released from skin cells, acting on vasodilating receptors in cutaneous vessels): Niacin activates a receptor on dermal Langerhans cells and keratinocytes, releasing prostaglandin D2 and E2 that dilate cutaneous vessels. Aspirin inhibits cyclooxygenase and so blunts the flush. Histamine, kinins, serotonin and substance P cause flushing in other settings but are not blocked by aspirin.
  93. The correct answer is C (Prerenal azotemia from volume depletion with preserved tubular function and avid sodium reabsorption): Volume loss lowers renal perfusion, and healthy tubules respond by retaining sodium and water, giving a low urine sodium, low fractional excretion of sodium, concentrated urine and a high BUN to creatinine ratio. Acute tubular necrosis would instead show isosthenuric urine with a high urine sodium and muddy brown casts, because injured tubules cannot reabsorb sodium. Prompt fluid repletion should reverse this injury.
  94. The correct answer is D (Focal seizure with impaired awareness arising from the mesial temporal lobe structures): An epigastric rising sensation, olfactory aura, oral automatisms, impaired awareness lasting more than a minute, and prolonged postictal confusion localize to the mesial temporal lobe, often with hippocampal sclerosis. Absence seizures are shorter, have no aura, and recover immediately. Frontal seizures are brief and often nocturnal.
  95. The correct answer is B (Substitution of valine for glutamic acid at the sixth amino acid position): Hemoglobin S results from a missense mutation replacing glutamic acid with valine at position 6 of the beta chain, creating a hydrophobic patch that promotes polymerization when deoxygenated. Substituting lysine at the same position produces hemoglobin C. Promoter, frameshift, and splice mutations cause thalassemia.
  96. The correct answer is D (100 liters, exceeding total body water and reflecting extensive sequestration outside plasma): The apparent volume equals the dose divided by the initial concentration, so 500 mg divided by 5 mg/L gives 100 L. That exceeds the roughly 42 L of total body water, which means most drug sits in tissue rather than plasma. Drugs confined to plasma show volumes near 3 L.
  97. The correct answer is D (Left lateral recumbent or seated positioning so the uterus does not compress the inferior vena cava): In late pregnancy the supine position lets the uterus compress the inferior vena cava, lowering venous return, cardiac output and blood pressure. Moving to a left lateral or seated position relieves the compression and can be used for most techniques. Leg elevation while supine leaves the uterine compression in place.
  98. The correct answer is B (T5 to T9 paraspinal segments, which carry sympathetic outflow to the stomach and upper gastrointestinal tract): Sympathetic preganglionic fibers to the stomach arise from about T5 to T9, so chronic gastric dysfunction produces paraspinal texture change at those levels. The vagus supplies parasympathetic tone but does not create sympathetic paraspinal findings, and the other levels belong to the head, proximal colon, kidney, distal colon and pelvic organs.
  99. The correct answer is A (Left medial longitudinal fasciculus lesion interrupting abducens to oculomotor internuclear signals): A lesion of the medial longitudinal fasciculus between the abducens interneurons and the contralateral oculomotor nucleus produces internuclear ophthalmoplegia: the eye on the side of the lesion fails to adduct, and the abducting eye shows nystagmus. Convergence bypasses this pathway, so adduction is preserved when convergence is tested. An oculomotor nerve palsy would also impair convergence and cause ptosis or mydriasis. In a young woman, demyelination is the usual cause.
  100. The correct answer is D (Classical Hodgkin lymphoma of the nodular sclerosis type, the commonest subtype in young women): A young woman with an anterior mediastinal mass and lacunar cells within a background of fibrous collagen bands has nodular sclerosis classical Hodgkin lymphoma, the most common subtype. The distinguishing detail is the lacunar morphology with banded fibrosis. Primary mediastinal B-cell lymphoma also sclerosis-associated but shows CD20 positive cells rather than CD15 and CD30 positive cells.

Practice Test 5 Answer Key

  1. The correct answer is E (C5 extended, rotated right, sidebent right, named for the position of ease): A somatic dysfunction is named for the direction of freedom, the position of ease, so a segment moving freely into extension, right sidebending, and right rotation is extended, rotated right, sidebent right. Naming for the restricted directions reverses the diagnosis, and C2 to C7 follow type II mechanics with sidebending and rotation to the same side.
  2.
