Etomidate is also hemodynamically stable and is a defensible alternative in shock, but it gives no airway benefit, while propofol and thiopental would worsen hypotension.
  72. The correct answer is D (Weak dorsiflexion and eversion with preserved inversion, plus numbness over the dorsal foot): The common peroneal nerve is compressed at the fibular neck by tight casts, weakening dorsiflexion and eversion and causing foot drop with dorsal foot numbness, while inversion by the tibial nerve remains. Tibial nerve injury would weaken plantarflexion and inversion with sole numbness. Femoral, obturator, and superior gluteal injuries affect proximal movements.
  73. The correct answer is A (Squatting from standing, which raises venous return and afterload and widens the outflow tract): A harsh murmur that softens supine with a family history of sudden death fits dynamic outflow obstruction from hypertrophic cardiomyopathy. Squatting increases venous return and afterload, enlarging the ventricle and widening the outflow tract, which softens the murmur. Strain, standing, and vasodilators reduce preload or afterload and increase the obstruction.
  74. The correct answer is D (Constipation, because enteric mu receptors resist desensitization and keep slowing intestinal transit): Tolerance develops to nausea, sedation, euphoria, and respiratory depression, but little develops to constipation because enteric mu receptors remain responsive. A scheduled stimulant laxative is therefore started with chronic opioids. Respiratory depression is the tempting choice, but tolerance does diminish it with gradual titration.
  75. The correct answer is A (Bronchogenic carcinoma, because asbestos and tobacco smoke act synergistically to multiply lung cancer risk): Lung cancer is the most common malignancy in asbestos-exposed workers, and smoking multiplies the risk many fold, so his risk of bronchogenic carcinoma far exceeds his risk of mesothelioma. Mesothelioma is strongly specific to asbestos but is much less common and shows no interaction with smoking.
  76. The correct answer is E (Mohs micrographic surgery with full horizontal margin assessment during staged tissue removal): Recurrent, infiltrative or high-risk facial basal cell carcinoma is best treated by Mohs surgery, which examines the entire margin and conserves tissue while giving the highest cure rate. Curettage and imiquimod suit small superficial lesions, and standard excision has already failed. Radiation is reserved for patients who cannot have surgery.
  77. The correct answer is A (Nintedanib, a tyrosine kinase inhibitor that blocks fibroblast proliferation and profibrotic growth factor signaling): Honeycombing at the lung bases with traction bronchiectasis, clubbing and restriction indicate idiopathic pulmonary fibrosis with a usual interstitial pneumonia pattern. Antifibrotic drugs such as nintedanib and pirfenidone slow the loss of forced vital capacity. Immunosuppression with prednisone, azathioprine and N-acetylcysteine increased adverse outcomes in a trial.
  78. The correct answer is C (Right crus to L1 through L3 and left crus to L1 and L2): The right crus is longer and attaches to the anterior bodies of L1 to L3, while the left crus attaches to L1 to L2. The reversed pairing is a common slip. Because the crura reach the upper lumbar spine, dysfunction there can feed back into diaphragm motion and reflux symptoms.
  79. The correct answer is A (Left gonadal vein joins the left renal vein at a right angle, transmitting higher pressure): The left gonadal vein drains into the left renal vein at a nearly right angle, so venous pressure is transmitted back to the pampiniform plexus and valves become incompetent, producing a varicocele. The right gonadal vein enters the inferior vena cava obliquely at lower pressure. Lymphatics drain to the para-aortic nodes and do not form this mass.
  80. The correct answer is E (Testing for latent tuberculosis, because blocking tumor necrosis factor impairs granuloma maintenance): Tumor necrosis factor inhibitors impair granuloma maintenance, so latent tuberculosis can reactivate and every patient needs screening with an interferon gamma release assay or tuberculin test before treatment. Folate depletion belongs to methotrexate, which she already takes. Thiopurine methyltransferase testing applies to azathioprine rather than to biologics.
  81. The correct answer is A (Left Meyer loop of the optic radiation, carrying inferior retinal fibers through the temporal lobe): Inferior retinal fibers see the upper visual field and loop through the temporal lobe as Meyer loop, so a left temporal lesion causes a right superior quadrantanopia in both eyes, called pie in the sky. The parietal radiation carries superior retinal fibers and its lesion causes an inferior defect, while chiasmal lesions give bitemporal defects.
  82. The correct answer is B (Deficient metalloprotease activity leaving ultralarge von Willebrand multimers to form platelet-rich microthrombi): Fever, neurologic change, renal injury, microangiopathic hemolysis and severe thrombocytopenia with normal coagulation studies indicate thrombotic thrombocytopenic purpura. The cause is deficient ADAMTS13, which normally cleaves ultralarge von Willebrand multimers. Disseminated intravascular coagulation would prolong the PT and aPTT and lower fibrinogen.
  83. The correct answer is C (Absent ganglion cells in submucosal and myenteric plexuses with hypertrophied nerve trunks): Hirschsprung disease results from failed neural crest migration, leaving the distal bowel without ganglion cells in both plexuses and with thick extrinsic nerve fibers that stain for acetylcholinesterase. The rectum is always involved and the transition zone is the dilated normal bowel above. Muscle hypertrophy and granulomas describe other diseases.
  84. The correct answer is B (Radial nerve, lying directly against bone within the spiral groove of the arm): The radial nerve lies against the humerus in the spiral groove, so a midshaft fracture causes wrist drop and loss of sensation on the dorsal first web space. Triceps is spared because its branches leave the nerve proximal to the groove, and a posterior interosseous lesion would spare skin sensation.
  85. The correct answer is C (Cytomegalovirus, a herpesvirus shed in child saliva and urine, infecting the fetus across the placenta): Periventricular calcifications with microcephaly, petechiae and sensorineural hearing loss point to congenital cytomegalovirus, the most common congenital infection, often acquired from young children's secretions. Toxoplasmosis typically gives scattered calcifications with hydrocephalus and chorioretinitis, and the absent cat exposure further argues against it. Rubella would add cataracts and a patent ductus.
  86. The correct answer is B (Aspirate the bursa for cell count, Gram stain and culture, then begin antistaphylococcal antibiotics): A warm, fluctuant bursa with fever after a skin break suggests septic olecranon bursitis, usually from Staphylococcus aureus. Aspiration with culture confirms the diagnosis and antistaphylococcal antibiotics follow. Preserved elbow motion shows the joint is not infected, so joint aspiration is unnecessary, and steroid injection would be harmful.
  87. The correct answer is B (Impaired hydroxylation of proline and lysine residues, preventing stable triple helix assembly of procollagen): Perifollicular hemorrhages, corkscrew hairs and bleeding gums in a man with no fruit or vegetables indicate scurvy. Vitamin C is a cofactor for prolyl and lysyl hydroxylases, so procollagen cannot form a stable triple helix and wounds fail to hold. Copper dependent crosslinking defects occur in a different disorder and would not fit this diet.
  88. The correct answer is E (White blood cell casts formed within renal tubules and carried into the urine): White cell casts take the shape of renal tubules, so they prove the leukocytes came from the kidney rather than the bladder, confirming acute pyelonephritis. Leukocyte esterase, nitrite and bacteria occur in cystitis too. Squamous cells indicate contamination, and eosinophils point to interstitial nephritis.
  89. The correct answer is E (Magnetic resonance imaging, with protected weight bearing and evaluation for low energy availability): Rest pain, night pain, focal bony tenderness, and menstrual dysfunction with low body weight suggest a tibial stress fracture in a patient with the female athlete triad. Radiographs are insensitive early, so MRI is the best next test while avoiding loading. Shin splints produce diffuse pain that does not occur at rest.
  90. The correct answer is C (Ureteric bud, which induces metanephric mesenchyme to form nephrons and gives rise to collecting ducts): The ureteric bud sprouts from the mesonephric duct, invades the metanephric mesenchyme and induces nephron formation, so failure of the bud yields absence of the kidney and ureter. The metanephric mesenchyme is a separate intermediate mesoderm derivative and cannot explain a missing ureter.
  91. The correct answer is E (Hepatocellular carcinoma arising from malignant transformation of regenerating hepatocytes after years of viral inflammation): In a cirrhotic liver, a mass with arterial hyperenhancement and portal venous washout, together with a markedly raised alpha-fetoprotein, is diagnostic of hepatocellular carcinoma. Cholangiocarcinoma is the main competing primary tumor but shows delayed, progressive enhancement and rises in CA 19-9 rather than alpha-fetoprotein. Metformin use is incidental.
  92. The correct answer is C (Galbreath mandibular drainage technique, using gentle lateral traction on the angle of the mandible): The Galbreath technique applies gentle traction at the mandible angle to assist Eustachian tube function and lymphatic drainage in children with otitis media with effusion when no acute infection is present. Frontal lift targets the sinuses, venous sinus technique targets intracranial venous flow, and the other cranial techniques do not directly influence the Eustachian tube.
  93. The correct answer is D (Fine-tune the position with small adjustments until tenderness falls by about seventy percent): A reduction of only twenty percent is short of the goal, which is roughly a seventy percent fall in tenderness. The physician fine-tunes with small adjustments and then holds the final position about ninety seconds. Moving to the barrier, compressing or asking for contraction does not meet the criteria of the technique.
  94. The correct answer is B (Paraumbilical veins of the portal system communicating with the superficial epigastric veins): Portal hypertension reopens the paraumbilical veins within the falciform ligament, which drain to the superficial epigastric veins of the systemic circulation and produce caput medusae. The left gastric to azygos and superior to middle rectal anastomoses cause varices at the esophagus and anorectum instead.
  95. The correct answer is A (Inhibition of xanthine oxidase, reducing conversion of hypoxanthine and xanthine to uric acid): Allopurinol, the usual initial drug for lowering urate, inhibits xanthine oxidase so less hypoxanthine and xanthine is converted to uric acid. Uricosurics act on URAT1 and are second line, colchicine and NSAIDs treat or prevent flares without lowering urate, and pegloticase is reserved for refractory disease. Thiazides raise uric acid by competing for tubular secretion.
  96. The correct answer is D (Myocardium affected by coronary ischemia, with sympathetic afferents entering the cord at T1 to T5): The sympathetic supply of the heart arises from T1 to T5, so segmental tissue changes at T2 to T4 on the left point to a cardiac source, in keeping with the exertional pressure. Stomach reflexes occur lower, at T5 to T9, and would not explain upper thoracic findings.
  97. The correct answer is C (Intravenous lorazepam titrated to symptoms, enhancing GABA-A activity to control delirium and prevent seizures): Fever, autonomic instability, hallucinations and disorientation three to four days after the final drink indicate delirium tremens, which carries real mortality if untreated. Benzodiazepines potentiate GABA-A and treat both the delirium and the seizure risk. Haloperidol may calm hallucinations but lowers the seizure threshold and does not treat the underlying withdrawal.
  98. The correct answer is C (Apply a thumb spica splint and obtain MRI or repeat films in about ten days): Snuffbox tenderness after a fall on an outstretched hand suggests a scaphoid fracture even when early radiographs are normal, since fractures are often occult. Empiric thumb spica immobilization protects against nonunion and avascular necrosis while MRI or repeat radiographs at 10 to 14 days confirm the injury. Surgery needs a confirmed displaced fracture.
  99. The correct answer is D (Stretch of ventricular myocytes from raised wall tension and elevated filling pressure): B-type natriuretic peptide is secreted by ventricular myocytes when wall stretch from pressure or volume overload rises, and it promotes natriuresis and vasodilation. The adrenal, juxtaglomerular, hypothalamic, and renal fibroblast stimuli each drive a different hormone and do not release this peptide.
  100. The correct answer is A (Azithromycin, a 50S ribosomal inhibitor active against an organism that lacks a peptidoglycan wall): This is walking pneumonia from Mycoplasma pneumoniae, which has no cell wall, so it stains poorly on Gram stain and is resistant to beta-lactams. Macrolides, doxycycline or respiratory fluoroquinolones are active, and azithromycin is the usual choice for a young adult. Cold agglutinins are IgM antibodies against the I antigen on red cells and cause a mild, usually self-limited hemolysis.

Practice Test 7 Answer Key

  1. The correct answer is E (Annual low-dose chest CT, because his age, smoking history, and time since cessation meet screening criteria): Annual low-dose CT reduces lung cancer mortality in people aged 50 to 80 with at least a 20 pack-year history who currently smoke or quit within the past 15 years. This man meets each criterion with 25 pack-years and 8 years since quitting. Radiograph and sputum cytology do not reduce mortality, and a 5 year interval is not recommended.
  2. The correct answer is E (Cyclooxygenase blockade diverts arachidonic acid toward leukotriene synthesis, which drives bronchoconstriction and mucosal edema): Aspirin-exacerbated respiratory disease is triggered by cyclooxygenase inhibition, which pushes arachidonic acid down the lipoxygenase pathway and increases cysteinyl leukotrienes. The reaction is not IgE-mediated, so it occurs on first exposure and cross-reacts among NSAIDs. A leukotriene receptor antagonist can help treat it.
  3. The correct answer is C (Psychogenic erectile dysfunction related to performance anxiety, with intact vascular, neural, and hormonal pathways): Preserved morning and masturbatory erections show that the vascular, neural, and hormonal pathways are working, so situational loss of erections in a young man under stress is psychogenic. Organic causes usually reduce all erections, and hypogonadism would also lower libido and morning erections.
  4. The correct answer is C (Right superior gluteal nerve, supplying gluteus medius and minimus on the stance side): The pelvis drops on the side opposite the weak abductors when standing on one leg, so a dropped left pelvis while standing on the right means right gluteus medius and minimus are weak. Their supply is the superior gluteal nerve, which is endangered by injections placed medial to the upper outer quadrant.
  5. The correct answer is D (Reduced apolipoprotein synthesis impairing export of triglycerides from hepatocytes as lipoprotein particles): In kwashiorkor, dietary protein is inadequate relative to calories, so the liver cannot make enough apolipoproteins to package and export triglycerides, producing a fatty, enlarged liver. The low albumin on the panel explains the edema through reduced oncotic pressure. Heart failure or glycogen storage disease would not be expected with this diet and laboratory profile.
  6. The correct answer is D (Anti-Smith antibody, directed against core proteins of small nuclear ribonucleoprotein particles): Anti-Smith antibodies are highly specific for systemic lupus erythematosus even though only about a quarter of patients have them. Anti-histone antibodies point to drug induced lupus, which this patient with a malar rash and oral ulcers without a culprit drug does not suggest. The scleroderma and antisynthetase antibodies belong to different diseases.
  7. The correct answer is A (1 in 150, from two thirds carrier probability times partner carrier frequency times one fourth): An unaffected sibling of a person with a recessive disease has a two thirds chance of being a carrier, since the affected genotype is excluded. The risk is two thirds times one in 25 times one fourth, which is one in 150. Treating her as a certain carrier overstates the risk to one in 100.
  8. The correct answer is D (Deposition of IgA containing immune complexes in small vessel walls of skin, gut and kidney): The tetrad of palpable purpura, arthralgia, abdominal pain and hematuria after an infection describes IgA vasculitis, caused by IgA immune complex deposition in small vessels. Platelets are normal because the purpura reflects vessel inflammation rather than thrombocytopenia.
  9. The correct answer is A (Pancreatic exocrine insufficiency reducing lipase delivery while the intestinal mucosa absorbs normally): Low fecal elastase with steatorrhea in chronic alcohol use points to pancreatic exocrine insufficiency, and a normal D-xylose test shows the mucosa absorbs the pentose normally. Celiac disease and Whipple disease damage the mucosa and make D-xylose absorption abnormal.
  10. The correct answer is E (Alpha-1A receptor blockade relaxing prostatic smooth muscle and the bladder neck within days): Smooth benign enlargement with obstructive symptoms is benign prostatic hyperplasia, and alpha-1 blockers such as tamsulosin relax the prostate and bladder neck within days, matching his timeline. A 5-alpha reductase inhibitor shrinks the gland but takes months. Antimuscarinic drugs risk retention in obstruction.
  11. The correct answer is A (Part B, supplementary medical insurance funded by premiums and general revenue, covering clinic care and screening): Part B is the supplementary medical insurance that covers physician visits, outpatient care and many preventive services, with a monthly premium. Part A funds inpatient care and is premium free for those with enough work credits. Medigap only reimburses cost sharing and Part D covers drugs.
  12. The correct answer is D (Cohort study, because few workers carry this solvent history and can be enrolled directly): When the exposure is rare, a cohort study that deliberately enrolls exposed workers and a comparison group is efficient, because a case-control sample would contain very few exposed cases. The rarity of the tumor tempts toward a case-control design, but it is the rarity of the solvent history that decides. Randomly assigning a harmful solvent would be unethical.
  13. The correct answer is E (Carboxyhemoglobin absorbs light similarly to oxyhemoglobin, so the two-wavelength device overestimates true oxygen saturation): Standard pulse oximeters use only two wavelengths and cannot tell carboxyhemoglobin from oxyhemoglobin, so saturation looks normal while real oxygen-carrying capacity is reduced. The PO2 is also normal because it reflects dissolved oxygen, not hemoglobin saturation. Co-oximetry on blood confirms the diagnosis and the patient needs 100% oxygen. Cyanide may also cause lactic acidosis in fires, but it does not change the oximeter reading.
  14. The correct answer is A (Glucose-6-phosphatase deficiency blocking the final step of both gluconeogenesis and glycogenolysis in liver): Without glucose-6-phosphatase, glucose-6-phosphate is trapped in hepatocytes, so neither glycogenolysis nor gluconeogenesis can release free glucose, which explains fasting hypoglycemia unresponsive to glucagon. The trapped intermediate is shunted into glycolysis and the pentose pathway, raising lactate, uric acid and triglycerides. Hers disease also causes hypoglycemia but lacks the marked lactic acidosis and hyperuricemia.
  15. The correct answer is E (Reduced responsiveness of collecting duct cells to antidiuretic hormone, impairing aquaporin 2 insertion): Lithium enters collecting duct cells and interferes with the vasopressin signaling that inserts aquaporin 2, causing nephrogenic diabetes insipidus that does not respond to desmopressin. Central diabetes insipidus would correct with desmopressin, and lithium related hypercalcemia is a possible contributor but not the main mechanism.
  16. The correct answer is E (Zero-order elimination because enzyme saturation fixes the amount removed per unit time): Alcohol dehydrogenase becomes saturated at ordinary intoxicating concentrations, so a constant amount rather than a constant fraction is metabolized per hour. This gives a straight line decline on a linear plot. A first order process would show a falling hourly amount as the concentration drops.
  17. The correct answer is C (Closure of the pleuroperitoneal canal, whose failure lets viscera herniate and restrict lung growth): A scaphoid abdomen with bowel sounds in the chest and a shifted mediastinum indicates congenital diaphragmatic hernia, usually through the left posterolateral foramen of Bochdalek. Failure of the pleuroperitoneal membrane to close lets abdominal viscera enter the thorax during lung development, causing pulmonary hypoplasia. A tracheoesophageal fistula would instead cause feeding-related choking with a gas-filled abdomen.
  18. The correct answer is C (A single abdominal aortic ultrasound, because ever-smokers this age face higher aneurysm rupture risk): Men aged 65 to 75 who have ever smoked are offered one-time ultrasound screening for abdominal aortic aneurysm. He has about 15 pack-years and quit 18 years ago, so he does not meet lung cancer screening criteria. Screening carotid ultrasound, calcium scoring and resting ECG are not recommended in asymptomatic adults like him.
  19. The correct answer is B (Agonism at serotonin 1B and 1D receptors causing cranial vasoconstriction and reduced neuropeptide release): Triptans are agonists at 1B and 1D serotonin receptors, constricting dilated cranial vessels and inhibiting release of calcitonin gene-related peptide and other neuropeptides from trigeminal afferents. Beta blockers are used for prevention, not acute relief. Dopamine blockers treat the nausea but not the headache mechanism itself.
  20. The correct answer is C (It carries a small risk of progression to invasive squamous cell carcinoma over time): Actinic keratosis is a premalignant keratinocyte dysplasia caused by cumulative ultraviolet damage. Most lesions do not progress, but a small proportion become invasive squamous cell carcinoma. It does not arise from melanocytes and is not a precursor of basal cell carcinoma.
  21. The correct answer is D (The newer agent has a therapeutic index of 5 compared with 3 for the comparator): The therapeutic index is the median toxic dose divided by the median effective dose: 100 divided by 20 gives 5, and 30 divided by 10 gives 3. A higher value means a wider margin between the dose that works and the dose that harms. A lower effective dose reflects potency and says nothing about safety.
  22. The correct answer is C (Tyrosine, because its usual synthesis from phenylalanine is blocked by the defective hydroxylase enzyme): Phenylalanine hydroxylase converts phenylalanine to tyrosine, so when it is deficient tyrosine cannot be made and becomes conditionally essential. Tryptophan is tempting because tetrahydrobiopterin is a shared cofactor, but tryptophan is already an essential dietary amino acid and its supply is not affected by this block.
  23. The correct answer is D (Cyproheptadine, a serotonin 5-HT2A receptor antagonist given orally or by nasogastric tube): Hyperreflexia, inducible clonus, diarrhea, and fever after adding tramadol to an SSRI indicate serotonin syndrome. When benzodiazepines are not enough, the serotonin antagonist cyproheptadine is given, and antipyretics do not work because the heat comes from muscle hyperactivity rather than a raised set point.
  24. The correct answer is D (Start phenoxybenzamine for alpha blockade and add a beta blocker only after adequate alpha blockade): Elevated metanephrines with an adrenal mass indicate pheochromocytoma, and the first step is alpha blockade, typically phenoxybenzamine, to prevent hypertensive crisis. A beta blocker is added only afterward, because beta blockade alone leaves unopposed alpha stimulation. Patients also need liberal sodium intake, not restriction or diuretics.
  25. The correct answer is A (Supination, since the head cannot translate forward over the humeral capitulum): A radial head sitting posteriorly cannot glide forward, and supination moves it anteriorly, so supination is restricted. Pronation moves the head backward and stays free. Elbow flexion involves the humeroulnar joint and is not the motion limited by this finding.
  26. The correct answer is E (T10 to L1 paraspinal region, corresponding to sympathetic supply of the kidney and the ureter): The kidney and ureter have sympathetic viscerosomatic reflexes at about T10 to L1, and acute disease produces warm, edematous, boggy tissue there. The bladder reflex sits slightly lower at T12 to L2, which is tempting because the pain radiates toward the groin. The absence of voiding symptoms points away from the bladder levels.
  27. Wavy fibers and hypereosinophilia occur in the first hours, whereas macrophages and granulation tissue appear after the first week.
  28. Penicillin treats streptococcal disease, which can mimic the rash and adenopathy but does not alter the vasculitis.
  29. The correct answer is E (Structure and function are reciprocally interrelated, so rib cage mechanics shape respiratory performance): The tenet that structure and function are reciprocally interrelated links a restricted, rigid rib cage to impaired ventilatory mechanics, so improving rib motion can improve function. The unity and self-regulation tenets are true statements but do not specifically justify treating the chest wall, and osteopathic treatment complements medical therapy rather than replacing it.

This is a surgical emergency. Unilateral root, peripheral nerve, and plexus lesions do not explain the sphincter and perineal findings.
  31. Tissue diagnosis by biopsy is required. Candidiasis scrapes off, and watchful waiting would delay diagnosis of a premalignant or malignant lesion.
  32. The correct answer is C (Chlorinated vinyl compound exposure linked to malignant transformation of hepatic sinusoidal endothelium): Hepatic angiosarcoma is the classic tumor linked to vinyl chloride exposure in polymer plant workers. Aflatoxin also targets the liver but causes hepatocellular carcinoma from hepatocytes, whereas this biopsy shows malignant endothelial cells.
  33. Mild chronic hyponatremia without neurologic symptoms is treated with fluid restriction. Isotonic saline can worsen the sodium because the excess solute is excreted in a small urine volume while free water is retained.
  34. Nystatin is active against yeast but not dermatophytes, and steroid alone worsens the infection; oral therapy is reserved for extensive, scalp or nail disease.
  35. The correct answer is D (Extend the trunk and fine-tune toward comfort until tenderness drops, then hold about ninety seconds): Posterior tender points are generally treated with extension, and counterstrain positions the tissue toward ease, fine-tuning until tenderness falls by most of its starting level, and holds that position about ninety seconds. Anterior points use flexion, and active contractions or springing belong to other techniques.
  36. The correct answer is E (Dilated eye examination at diagnosis, then every one to two years if the first is normal): Type 2 diabetes may be present for years before diagnosis, so retinopathy can already exist and a dilated examination is done at diagnosis. If it is normal and glycemia is controlled, repeat examinations can be spaced to every one to two years. The five year delay applies to type 1 diabetes, and waiting for visual symptoms misses early disease.
  37. The correct answer is E (2300 mL, calculated as expiratory reserve volume plus residual volume of the lungs): The volume left after a quiet breath out is the functional residual capacity, equal to expiratory reserve volume plus residual volume, or 1100 plus 1200 mL. Vital capacity is 4600 mL and total lung capacity is 5800 mL. Inspiratory capacity of 3500 mL describes air that can be inhaled, not air that remains.
  38. Kernicterus produces athetoid movements and hearing loss, and spinal muscular atrophy would give flaccid weakness with hyporeflexia.
  39. The correct answer is C (21-hydroxylase deficiency blocking cortisol and aldosterone synthesis while diverting precursors into androgen production): A markedly raised 17-hydroxyprogesterone with virilization and salt wasting is classic for 21-hydroxylase deficiency. Cortisol and aldosterone are low, and precursors flow to androgens. Deficiency of 11-beta-hydroxylase also virilizes but retains salt and raises blood pressure.
  40. The correct answer is D (Fetal alcohol spectrum disorder from prenatal ethanol exposure with characteristic facial and growth findings): Short palpebral fissures, a smooth philtrum, a thin upper lip, growth restriction, microcephaly and behavioral problems define the fetal alcohol spectrum, and a septal defect fits the associated cardiac anomalies. Ethanol is a leading preventable cause of intellectual disability and has no known safe threshold in pregnancy. Williams and Fragile X have different facial features and genetic mechanisms.
  41. Transketolase of the pentose phosphate pathway needs thiamine pyrophosphate, as do pyruvate dehydrogenase and alpha-ketoglutarate dehydrogenase.
  42. Tarsal tunnel syndrome would give burning paresthesias and a positive Tinel sign. A stress fracture is tender with lateral squeeze and tends to worsen with continued loading.
  43. The correct answer is E (Enrolling patients with more severe disease so the expected treatment effect is larger): Power rises with a larger expected effect, and enrolling patients with more severe disease can increase the treatment effect that can be detected. Splitting alpha across secondary outcomes or adding a third arm lowers power, and a smaller target difference reduces it. Blinding reduces bias but does not change the ability to detect a true difference.
  44. It also inhibits rRNA degradation, producing basophilic stippling. The other pairs belong to different porphyrias or to heme catabolism.
  45. The correct answer is A (About 95 percent, since the interval extends two SDs above and below the average): The limits of 160 and 240 are each two standard deviations from the mean of 200, and roughly 95 percent of a normal distribution lies within two standard deviations. One standard deviation would enclose about 68 percent and three would enclose about 99.7 percent.
  46. The correct answer is E (Expressive language, since about 50 words and two-word combinations are expected by two years): By two years a child typically has about 50 words and begins combining two words, so about 10 isolated words is an expressive language delay. Comprehension, pointing, and motor skills are age-appropriate here, which makes a global or motor delay unlikely. Normal hearing screening points toward an isolated speech delay that merits early language evaluation.
  47. The correct answer is B (Sensitivity rises and specificity falls, producing more false positives and fewer false negatives): Lowering the cutoff calls more samples positive, which catches more truly diseased children (higher sensitivity) but also labels more healthy children positive (lower specificity). The number of false positives therefore rises and false negatives fall. Positive predictive value would, if anything, fall rather than rise.
  48. Urine reducing substances are positive while glucose oxidase testing is negative because galactose is not detected by it. Galactokinase deficiency causes cataracts alone and lacks liver failure.
  49. The correct answer is B (C1 on C2 rotated right, named for the direction in which the segment moves freely): Full flexion locks the lower cervical segments, so a rotation deficit during this test points to C1 on C2, whose primary motion is rotation. Restricted left rotation means free right rotation, and the dysfunction is named for freedom, so it is rotated right.
  50. The correct answer is A (Biliary atresia with progressive obliteration of the extrahepatic bile ducts): Conjugated hyperbilirubinemia with acholic stools, a high GGT, and a nonvisualized gallbladder in a well-appearing infant points to biliary atresia, which needs surgical portoenterostomy early. Neonatal hepatitis usually allows a visible gallbladder, and a choledochal cyst would show cystic dilation on imaging. Delay beyond about two months worsens outcome.
  51. The correct answer is C (Postoperative atelectasis from hypoventilation and mucus plugging causing collapse with lobar volume loss): Atelectasis is a volume-loss process, so the trachea and heart are pulled toward the opaque side, and postoperative splinting with retained secretions is the classic setting. Effusion and tension pneumothorax both push the mediastinum away from the lesion. Pneumonia alone consolidates without volume loss and does not shift the mediastinum.
  52. The correct answer is C (Spironolactone, an aldosterone receptor antagonist that limits myocardial fibrosis and adverse ventricular remodeling): Aldosterone antagonists block mineralocorticoid driven fibrosis and sodium retention and lower mortality when added to an ACE inhibitor and beta blocker in reduced ejection fraction. Digoxin reduces admissions but does not lower death. Amlodipine is neutral at best, and ivabradine lowers hospitalization without a mortality benefit.
  53. The correct answer is B (Interleukin-6 driven hepcidin release trapping iron within macrophages and blocking intestinal absorption): Inflammation raises interleukin-6, which stimulates hepcidin; hepcidin degrades ferroportin, trapping iron in macrophages and limiting absorption. This yields high ferritin, low iron, and low binding capacity, typical of anemia of chronic disease. Blood loss would lower ferritin, and folate supplementation argues against methotrexate related macrocytosis.
  54. The correct answer is D (Reduced GABA and acetylcholine in the striatum with relative excess of dopamine activity): Huntington disease, an autosomal dominant CAG repeat disorder, destroys striatal medium spiny neurons, reducing GABA and acetylcholine while dopaminergic input is relatively overactive, which produces chorea. Imaging typically shows caudate atrophy with enlarged frontal horns. Parkinson disease shows the opposite dopamine and acetylcholine balance.
  55. The correct answer is E (Arrange time alone with her, then ask direct, nonjudgmental questions about safety at home): Patients are usually safest when screened alone, so the physician should create a routine reason to separate them and then ask directly. Confronting the partner or offering couples therapy can raise the risk of retaliation. Repeated vague complaints with bruises of different ages should prompt screening, not repeated testing or medication.
  56. The correct answer is E (Gluconeogenesis, since pyruvate and oxaloacetate are diverted to lactate and malate by excess NADH): Ethanol oxidation by alcohol and acetaldehyde dehydrogenase floods hepatocytes with NADH, driving pyruvate to lactate and oxaloacetate to malate, so substrates for gluconeogenesis are unavailable. Glycogen is already depleted after two days of fasting, so glycogenolysis cannot rescue glucose. The lactate and ketone elevations in the panel fit this redox shift.
  57. The correct answer is C (Stomach viscerosomatic reflex through sympathetic input to facilitated cord segments at roughly T5 to T9): Postprandial epigastric burning with a positive urea breath test indicates gastric disease, and stomach sympathetic afferents are linked to cord segments at roughly T5 to T9, which matches the paraspinal findings. The vagus is a cranial parasympathetic supply to the stomach and does not act through sacral segments.
  58. The correct answer is A (Black pigment stones of calcium bilirubinate, formed from excess unconjugated bilirubin in chronic hemolysis): Chronic hemolysis loads bile with unconjugated bilirubin, which precipitates with calcium into black pigment stones in the gallbladder. Cholesterol stones follow cholesterol supersaturation, as in obesity. Brown stones need bacterial beta-glucuronidase from infection, usually in bile ducts.
  59. The correct answer is A (Degeneration of cortical upper motor neurons together with spinal anterior horn lower motor neurons): Combined lower motor neuron signs (wasting, fasciculations) and upper motor neuron signs (hyperreflexia, extensor plantars, spasticity) with no sensory or sphincter involvement define amyotrophic lateral sclerosis. Both cortical and anterior horn motor neurons degenerate. Poliovirus affects only anterior horn cells, and syringomyelia produces sensory loss.
  60. Intestinal type tumors arise through metaplasia with p53 changes or mismatch repair defects and form masses or ulcers rather than a rigid thickened wall.
  61. Diphenoxylate shares the same risk, and dose reduction does not remove it. The patient needs evaluation and targeted treatment rather than antimotility therapy.
  62. The correct answer is D (Hydrogen potassium ATPase on the secretory canalicular membrane of gastric parietal cells): Proton pump inhibitors are activated in acid and covalently and irreversibly bind the H+/K+ ATPase of the parietal cell canaliculus, so a single dose acts for more than a day until new pumps are synthesized. H2 blockers act reversibly at the histamine receptor and give shorter, less complete suppression.
  63. The correct answer is B (Depletion of pyridoxine through drug-induced impairment of its conversion to pyridoxal phosphate): Isoniazid interferes with the activation of pyridoxine, so patients develop a sensory peripheral neuropathy that is prevented by giving vitamin B6. Alcohol use and malnutrition raise the risk, which makes thiamine deficiency tempting, but the temporal link to isoniazid and the pyridoxine pathway make it the best answer. The drug inhibits mycolic acid in mycobacteria, not human myelin.
  64. The correct answer is E (Impaired mitochondrial beta-oxidation of medium-chain fatty acids, limiting acetyl-CoA supply for hepatic ketone body synthesis): In medium-chain acyl-CoA dehydrogenase deficiency, fatty acids cannot be oxidized to acetyl-CoA, so ketone bodies are not made and the fasting child becomes hypoglycemic without ketosis. Dicarboxylic aciduria results from omega-oxidation of unoxidized fatty acids. Hyperinsulinism also gives hypoketosis, but it suppresses lipolysis, so free fatty acids would be low rather than high.
  65. The correct answer is E (Excess dopaminergic transmission in the mesolimbic pathway from the ventral tegmentum to the nucleus accumbens): Hallucinations and delusions, the positive symptoms of schizophrenia, are attributed to overactivity of dopamine in the mesolimbic pathway, which is why D2 blockers relieve them. Reduced mesocortical dopamine is linked to negative and cognitive symptoms, and nigrostriatal and tuberoinfundibular effects explain drug side effects rather than the psychosis.
  66. The correct answer is D (A poxvirus that replicates in the cytoplasm of keratinocytes and forms Henderson-Patterson bodies): Molluscum contagiosum is caused by a poxvirus, which replicates in the cytoplasm and produces eosinophilic Henderson-Patterson bodies in the epidermis. Papillomavirus causes warts with koilocytes, and herpesviruses produce vesicles with multinucleated cells.
  67. The correct answer is C (Salmonella species, which invade infarcted bone in patients with functional asplenia): Bone infarcts and functional asplenia in sickle cell disease predispose to Salmonella osteomyelitis, although Staphylococcus aureus remains the single most common cause overall. Pseudomonas follows puncture wounds through the foot and Pasteurella follows animal bites. Tuberculosis would present far more indolently and usually in the spine.
  68. The correct answer is D (Diffuse effacement of podocyte foot processes with no discrete immune deposits): Heavy proteinuria without blood and with normal light microscopy in a young child is minimal change disease. Podocyte injury flattens the foot processes and loses the charge barrier, which responds well to corticosteroids. The other patterns involve visible deposits or basement membrane changes.
  69. Achalasia would show incomplete relaxation and absent peristalsis, and scleroderma would show a weak sphincter with no contractions.
  70. A normal corrected diffusing capacity places the problem outside the lung parenchyma, and severe obesity limits chest wall and diaphragmatic excursion, lowering expiratory reserve volume. Intrinsic interstitial disease would reduce diffusing capacity.
  71. The correct answer is D (Impaired enzymatic breakdown of bradykinin, increasing vascular permeability in the deep dermis and submucosa): Angiotensin converting enzyme normally degrades bradykinin, so an inhibitor lets it accumulate and leak fluid into deep tissue without itching or hives. This is why antihistamines fail. Hereditary C1 inhibitor deficiency is also bradykinin mediated but would not first appear in middle age after starting a new drug.
  72. The correct answer is B (Impaired hypothalamic hormone secretion from the posterior pituitary, with a preserved collecting duct response): Dilute urine after water deprivation with a rising serum osmolality shows the kidney is not conserving water, and a rise of more than 50 percent in urine osmolality after desmopressin places the defect in hormone release, as in central diabetes insipidus after head trauma. Nephrogenic disease would show little response to desmopressin.
  73. Reflux esophagitis is the main mimic, but it would be expected to improve with acid suppression.
  74. The correct answer is E (Abdominal malignancy such as gastric carcinoma, spreading through the thoracic duct to the venous angle): The thoracic duct drains the left side of the body and the abdomen and empties at the left venous angle, so a firm left supraclavicular node (Virchow node) points to an intra-abdominal cancer such as gastric carcinoma. The early satiety and weight loss fit. Head and neck malignancies would drain to higher cervical nodes, and breast cancer on the right would not reach the left side.

Hemispheric lesions cause ipsilateral limb dysmetria. Obstructive hydrocephalus from fourth ventricle compression accounts for the morning headaches and vomiting.
  76. The correct answer is A (Screening with an interferon gamma release assay, since TNF blockade impairs granuloma maintenance): TNF alpha is required to maintain granulomas, so blockade can reactivate latent tuberculosis, and testing for latent infection is mandatory before treatment even with a normal radiograph. Drug induced lupus is rare and does not require a baseline titer. Sputum studies and echocardiography target different problems.
  77. The correct answer is C (Degree of right ventricular outflow obstruction driving right-to-left shunting across the septal defect): In tetralogy of Fallot the ventricular septal defect is large and nonrestrictive, so ventricular pressures equalize and the direction of flow depends on relative resistance. The more severe the pulmonary outflow obstruction, the more blood shunts right to left and the deeper the cyanosis. Squatting raises systemic resistance and reduces that shunt.
  78. The correct answer is D (Small round blue cells forming Homer Wright rosettes, a primitive neuroectodermal tumor pattern): A midline cerebellar mass in a young child that seeds the subarachnoid space is classic for medulloblastoma, a small round blue cell tumor with Homer Wright rosettes. Pilocytic astrocytoma is cystic with a mural nodule and does not typically spread along the neuraxis, and ependymoma shows perivascular pseudorosettes rather than neuroblastic rosettes.
  79. The correct answer is D (Levator ani and coccygeus muscles stretching across the inferior opening of the pelvis): The pelvic diaphragm is formed by the levator ani and coccygeus, and it is the caudal transverse restriction through which venous and lymphatic return from the legs must pass. The perineal muscles belong to the urogenital triangle and lie inferior to this layer, which makes them a tempting but incorrect answer.
  80. The correct answer is C (Splitting, in which people are seen as all good or all bad without integration): Splitting is the characteristic defense of borderline personality disorder: others are idealized or devalued and the two views are not integrated, so opinions flip with perceived slights. Projection and displacement involve a different target or attribution, and the same person is the object of both extremes here.
  81. Her chronic tension-type headache and thoracic somatic dysfunction can be treated later. Treating the pattern as somatic or imaging the cervical spine alone delays the necessary head CT.
  82. The correct answer is C (Motor cortex and joint proprioceptor input driving medullary centers in proportion to muscular effort): During moderate exercise the arterial blood gas values stay normal, so ventilation is not being driven by changes in PO2, PCO2 or pH. The rise is matched to metabolic rate by feed-forward signals from the motor cortex and from muscle and joint receptors. Acidemia from lactate stimulates peripheral chemoreceptors only at heavy workloads above the anaerobic threshold.
  83. Trastuzumab injury is not dose related and often reverses. Cyclophosphamide, fluorouracil, and paclitaxel cause acute necrosis, vasospasm, and conduction effects respectively.
  84. The correct answer is B (Posteromedial papillary muscle, which has a single blood supply from the posterior descending artery): The posteromedial papillary muscle is supplied only by the posterior descending artery, whereas the anterolateral muscle has dual supply from the left anterior descending and circumflex branches, so inferior infarction preferentially ruptures the posteromedial muscle, giving acute severe mitral regurgitation. Septal rupture also causes a holosystolic murmur but at the left sternal border with a step-up in oxygen saturation.
  85. Spica casting and surgery are reserved for failure of harness treatment or later presentation. Triple diapers are not reliable treatment.
  86. The correct answer is D (Phenoxybenzamine, a nonselective alpha blocker, started days before surgery to control vasoconstriction and volume contraction): Catecholamine excess causes intense alpha-mediated vasoconstriction and chronic volume contraction, so an irreversible alpha blocker is started days before surgery and followed by liberal salt and fluid intake. A beta blocker may be added only after alpha blockade is established, because beta blockade alone leaves alpha stimulation unopposed and can precipitate a hypertensive crisis. Diuretics would deepen the volume depletion.
  87. Serotonin syndrome gives hyperreflexia and clonus, and a simple dystonic reaction lacks systemic features.
  88. Ulcerative colitis is continuous from the rectum and seldom forms fistulas. Tuberculosis has caseation, and ischemic and pseudomembranous colitis do not fit the history.
  89. The correct answer is C (Left anterior innominate rotation, producing an apparent lengthening of the left leg): Anterior rotation of the left innominate brings the ASIS down and the PSIS up, and the acetabulum moves inferiorly, so the left leg appears longer. Posterior rotation would raise the ASIS and lower the PSIS with an apparent shortening. A superior shear moves both landmarks upward rather than in opposite directions.
  90. The correct answer is C (Right unilateral sacral flexion, in which the sacral base moves anteriorly on that side): A positive seated flexion test with a deep sulcus and a posterior, inferior inferior lateral angle on the same side indicates a unilateral sacral flexion on that side. Extension would produce a shallow sulcus, and torsions or bilateral patterns do not fit findings confined to one side.
  91. The correct answer is E (Ankylosing spondylitis, an HLA-B27 associated enthesitis centered inflammation of the axial skeleton): Inflammatory back pain that improves with exercise, reduced chest expansion and bilateral sacroiliitis in a young man indicate ankylosing spondylitis. Psoriatic, reactive and enteropathic arthritis can cause sacroiliitis but the skin, nail, infectious and bowel clues are absent here. Diffuse idiopathic skeletal hyperostosis affects older adults and spares the sacroiliac joints.
  92. The correct answer is C (Exclusion of duodenum and proximal jejunum from food flow, plus low acid, reduces iron uptake): Dietary nonheme iron is absorbed mainly in the duodenum and proximal jejunum, in an acidic environment that keeps it in the ferrous form, and both conditions are reduced after Roux-en-Y bypass. Low ferritin with microcytosis indicates true iron deficiency, not anemia of inflammation. Vitamin B12 deficiency would cause macrocytosis and is excluded by the normal level.
  93. The correct answer is D (Mutation in COL1A1 or COL1A2 reducing the quantity or quality of type I collagen): Recurrent low-impact fractures with blue sclerae, dentinogenesis imperfecta and early hearing loss define osteogenesis imperfecta, most often from COL1A1 or COL1A2 mutations. Normal mineral chemistry rules out rickets or hypophosphatasia. Fibrillin and type III collagen defects cause Marfan and vascular Ehlers-Danlos syndromes, which do not feature recurrent fractures.
  94. The correct answer is A (Deficiency of terminal complement components, which prevents assembly of the membrane attack complex on Neisseria): Recurrent Neisseria infections with normal antibody levels and white cell counts point to a terminal complement defect, since the membrane attack complex made of C5 through C9 is the main defense against these organisms. Early component deficiencies cause lupus-like immune complex disease, C1 inhibitor deficiency causes angioedema, and a broad C3 deficiency produces many pyogenic infections.
  95. The correct answer is C (Intravenous esmolol to lower heart rate and the rate of rise of aortic pressure): Tearing pain, an arm pressure difference, and a widened mediastinum suggest aortic dissection, where the goal is to reduce shear stress by lowering heart rate and the rate of pressure rise. A beta blocker such as esmolol comes first, and a vasodilator is added only after rate control. Thrombolytics and heparin risk catastrophic bleeding.
  96. The correct answer is A (Gamma carboxylation of glutamate residues in clotting factors using a reduced vitamin cofactor form): Newborns have sterile guts and little stored vitamin K, so factors II, VII, IX, and X are not gamma carboxylated, prolonging both PT and PTT. Defective factor XIII cross linking gives normal clotting times, and von Willebrand defects spare the PT.
  97. The correct answer is E (Pore formation in distal tubular membranes causing proton back leak with potassium and magnesium wasting): Amphotericin B forms pores in tubular membranes, which allows protons to leak back and causes a distal renal tubular acidosis with an inappropriately high urine pH. The same membrane leak wastes potassium and magnesium, giving a normal anion gap acidosis. Aldosterone deficiency would raise potassium instead of lowering it.
  98. The correct answer is D (You want better control, yet past attempts felt discouraging; what would make a change worth trying?): Motivational interviewing uses reflective listening and open questions to draw out the patient's own reasons for change and explore ambivalence, here by naming both sides and inviting him to consider what would help. Directive warnings, advice lists and labeling his motivation as low confront or bypass his ambivalence and tend to increase resistance.
  99. The correct answer is C (Loss of PTCH1 function causing unchecked signaling through the hedgehog pathway): A pearly papule with rolled borders and telangiectasia with palisading basaloid cells is basal cell carcinoma. These tumors arise from loss of PTCH1 (or activating SMOH mutations), leading to unrestrained hedgehog signaling. BRAF, CDKN2A and KIT alterations belong mainly to melanoma, and TP53 mutations are more typical of squamous cell carcinoma.
  100. The correct answer is C (Pore formation in distal tubular cell membranes, which leaks potassium and magnesium into the urine): Amphotericin B binds sterols and forms pores, and in the distal nephron this causes tubular leak of potassium and magnesium along with renal vasoconstriction and a rise in creatinine. The findings mimic a diuretic effect, but the drug has no transporter target. Chills at the first infusion reflect cytokine release, a separate infusion reaction.

Practice Test 8 Answer Key

  1. The correct answer is E (Beta-2 adrenergic receptor stimulation raising cyclic AMP and inactivating myosin light chain kinase): Albuterol is a selective beta-2 agonist whose Gs-coupled receptor raises cyclic AMP, activating protein kinase A and inhibiting myosin light chain kinase, so airway smooth muscle relaxes. Anticholinergics, methylxanthines and leukotriene antagonists act through different targets. The reversible obstruction on spirometry fits asthma.
  2. The correct answer is B (Ileal outpouching from persistent vitelline duct tissue containing ectopic gastric mucosa that secretes acid): Painless brick-red hematochezia in a toddler with a technetium-avid focus in the right lower quadrant points to a Meckel diverticulum, a vitelline duct remnant whose ectopic gastric mucosa secretes acid and ulcerates the adjacent ileum. Intussusception presents with colicky pain and currant jelly stool, and juvenile polyps would not take up the isotope.
  3. The correct answer is A (Epidural hematoma from a torn middle meningeal artery beneath a temporal bone fracture): A brief loss of consciousness, a lucid interval, and then rapid deterioration with a blown pupil point to arterial bleeding between the skull and dura, classically from the middle meningeal artery under the thin pterion. A subdural bleed is venous, usually develops more slowly, and crosses suture lines. Subarachnoid and basal ganglia hemorrhages do not fit a young man with head trauma and a lucid interval.
  4. The correct answer is C (Counterstrain, an indirect method that places tissue in ease to quiet abnormal muscle spindle input): Locating a tender point and holding the positioned tissue in ease for about ninety seconds describes counterstrain. Functional technique and facilitated positional release are also indirect but use dynamic monitoring or compression. Muscle energy depends on patient effort against resistance, which this patient was not asked to give.
  5. The correct answer is B (About 28 hours, which equals four half-lives of roughly seven hours each): The half-life equals 0.693 times volume divided by clearance, or about 7 hours. Each half-life closes half of the remaining gap to steady state, so four half-lives reach about 94 percent. One half-life gives only 50 percent, and the ratio of volume to clearance is the time constant, not the half-life.
  6. The correct answer is D (Bacterial urease hydrolyzes urea to ammonia, alkalinizing urine and precipitating magnesium ammonium phosphate): A urea splitting organism such as Proteus, which swarms on agar, produces ammonia, raising urine pH and precipitating struvite, which grows into staghorn calculi. Uric acid stones need acidic urine and are radiolucent. Calcium oxalate and cystine stones are not driven by infection.
  7. The correct answer is E (Complex regional pain syndrome type I, a sympathetically maintained disorder following minor limb trauma): Disproportionate burning pain, allodynia, swelling, and temperature and color change after minor limb trauma, with normal nerve studies and imaging, define complex regional pain syndrome type I. Carpal tunnel syndrome follows median nerve distribution with abnormal studies. Fibromyalgia is diffuse, and Raynaud phenomenon is episodic and cold triggered.
  8. The correct answer is C (Second stage, which ends with delivery of the infant after full cervical dilation): The second stage begins at complete cervical dilation and ends with delivery of the infant, and pushing and descent occur during it. The first stage ended at full dilation, the third stage covers delivery of the placenta, and the immediate postpartum recovery period follows.
  9. The correct answer is A (Type II, a non-neutral vertebra whose sidebending and rotation couple toward the same side): A single vertebra held in flexion or extension follows type II mechanics, with sidebending and rotation to the same side. Type I applies to neutral group curves in which sidebending and rotation occur to opposite sides, and type III describes motion introduced in one plane modifying the others.
  10. The correct answer is D (Gut contents sustain epithelial growth and barrier function, limiting bacterial translocation across the mucosa): Enteral nutrition keeps enterocytes supplied with luminal fuel such as glutamine and stimulates trophic hormones, so villi and tight junctions are preserved. Prolonged parenteral feeding is associated with villous atrophy and greater bacterial translocation. Enteral feeding still requires attention to micronutrients, and the parenteral route, not the enteral one, bypasses the liver.
  11. The correct answer is E (Variable extrathoracic obstruction, where negative airway pressure on inspiration collapses the narrowed airway segment): Above the thoracic inlet, airway pressure on inspiration falls below the surrounding atmospheric pressure, so a compressible narrowed segment collapses during inhalation. This flattens the inspiratory limb only. Intrathoracic variable lesions flatten the expiratory limb, and fixed lesions flatten both.
  12. The correct answer is E (Alpha thalassemia trait from deletion of two alpha globin genes): Microcytosis with a high red cell count, a Mentzer index under 13 (62 divided by 5.8 is about 10.7), normal ferritin, and normal electrophoresis suggests alpha thalassemia trait. Beta thalassemia minor raises hemoglobin A2, and iron deficiency lowers ferritin. Hemoglobin C would show a variant band.
  13. The correct answer is D (Propylthiouracil, chosen to avoid first-trimester methimazole embryopathy, with a planned switch to methimazole afterward): Methimazole is associated with a recognizable embryopathy in early pregnancy, so propylthiouracil is preferred in the first trimester. Because propylthiouracil carries a risk of severe liver injury, therapy is usually changed to methimazole after organogenesis. Radioiodine is absolutely avoided in pregnancy.
  14. The correct answer is C (Naloxone has a shorter duration of action than the opioid, allowing renewed receptor activation): Naloxone is a competitive mu receptor antagonist with a duration of roughly one hour, shorter than many opioids, so sedation and respiratory depression can return and the patient needs observation or an infusion. Withdrawal would produce agitation, vomiting and diarrhea instead, and naloxone does not act at GABA receptors.
  15. The correct answer is B (Order a D-dimer assay, since a negative result safely rules out clot at low probability): In a low pretest probability patient, a highly sensitive D-dimer is the right first step because a negative result effectively excludes embolism and spares imaging. A positive result would then lead to CT pulmonary angiography. Starting heparin or obtaining a leg ultrasound first reverses the sensible order for this patient.
  16. The correct answer is B (Lumbar puncture with spectrophotometric analysis of centrifuged cerebrospinal fluid for bilirubin pigment): Sensitivity of CT for subarachnoid blood falls over days as blood is cleared, so a normal scan three days after a thunderclap onset does not exclude hemorrhage. Lumbar puncture detects xanthochromia, which appears hours after bleeding and persists for weeks. Triptans are contraindicated in this setting, and repeating the same scan adds little.
  17. The correct answer is D (Acute pericarditis with diffuse epicardial inflammation producing concave ST elevation and PR depression): Positional pleuritic pain after a viral illness with diffuse concave ST elevation and PR depression is acute pericarditis. Early repolarization lacks PR depression and the typical pain. A myocardial infarction would be territorial with reciprocal changes, and myocarditis would raise troponin.
  18. The correct answer is A (Hypoperfusion infarcts in border zones between anterior, middle and posterior cerebral artery territories): Global hypoperfusion injures end-zone cortex where distal branches of adjacent arteries meet. The anterior to middle junction produces proximal arm and leg weakness (man in a barrel), and the middle to posterior junction produces visuospatial and distance judgment problems. Embolic strokes follow a single territory and are rarely bilateral and symmetric.
  19. The correct answer is B (Rapid intravenous adenosine, transiently blocking atrioventricular nodal conduction to interrupt the reentrant circuit): A regular narrow-complex tachycardia without P waves in a stable patient is most consistent with AV nodal reentry, and when vagal maneuvers fail the next step is rapid IV adenosine, which briefly blocks the AV node and breaks the circuit. Cardioversion is reserved for instability, and digoxin acts too slowly to terminate the rhythm.
  20. The correct answer is D (Fibrillin 1 mutation causing excess transforming growth factor beta signaling and medial degeneration): Tall habitus, arachnodactyly, upward lens dislocation, and aortic root dilation define Marfan syndrome, caused by a fibrillin 1 mutation with excess transforming growth factor beta signaling. Vascular Ehlers Danlos involves type III collagen. Homocystinuria dislocates the lens downward and favors thrombosis, and the other defects do not produce this combination.
  21. The correct answer is D (Mycobacterium tuberculosis, because damaged phagocytes fail to contain latent organisms and permit reactivation): Silica is toxic to alveolar macrophages, which die and release fibrogenic mediators, and the loss of effective phagocyte function raises the risk of tuberculosis several fold, including reactivation. Eggshell calcification of hilar nodes and upper lobe nodules are classic findings. Silicosis does not cause CD4 depletion, so Pneumocystis is not the expected opportunist.
  22. The correct answer is E (Magnetic resonance imaging of the whole spine, since vertebral metastasis is the leading concern): Night pain, weight loss, age, focal bony tenderness, and a prior malignancy are red flags for spinal metastasis. MRI of the entire spine is the best study because it shows marrow infiltration and other levels of involvement. Plain films miss early disease, and delaying imaging for conservative care is inappropriate.
  23. The correct answer is E (Counterstrain using gentle passive positioning toward ease with no thrust or sustained leverage on the spine): Severe osteoporosis and warfarin make thrust and strong direct forces unsafe, so a gentle indirect method such as counterstrain is the best starting choice. The strongest tempting wrong choice is forceful muscle energy, since it needs maximal effort and torque on fragile bone.
  24. The correct answer is B (Tumor secretion of a peptide that activates the parathyroid hormone receptor in bone and kidney): Squamous cell lung cancer commonly makes parathyroid hormone related peptide, which binds the PTH receptor and raises calcium while suppressing true PTH and calcitriol. A negative bone scan argues against osteolytic metastases, and a low calcitriol level argues against a calcitriol driven mechanism.
  25. The correct answer is D (Vagus nerve, which slows pacemaker firing and exits with cranial nerves IX and XI): The vagus nerve provides parasympathetic input to the sinoatrial and atrioventricular nodes and leaves the skull at the jugular foramen with the glossopharyngeal and accessory nerves. The glossopharyngeal nerve shares the foramen and senses carotid sinus pressure but does not slow the pacemaker directly.
  26. The correct answer is A (Hydrochlorothiazide, which competes for tubular organic anion secretion and promotes volume depletion, reducing renal urate excretion): Thiazide diuretics reduce renal urate excretion by competing for secretory transporters and by volume contraction, which raises serum urate and can precipitate gout. Needle-shaped negatively birefringent crystals are monosodium urate. Losartan is mildly uricosuric, amlodipine has a small urate-lowering effect, and ACE inhibitors and spironolactone do not meaningfully raise urate.
  27. The correct answer is D (Wilcoxon rank-sum (Mann-Whitney) test, which compares ranks and does not rely on normality): With only eight patients per group and one extreme outlier, the normality assumption of the t test is not defensible. The rank-sum test uses ranks and is the nonparametric counterpart for two independent samples. The signed-rank test is the paired equivalent, and dichotomizing length of stay would throw away information.
  28. The correct answer is C (Respect her refusal and pursue other measures, such as endoscopic hemostasis, volume expanders, and iron): A capable adult may refuse any treatment, including transfusion, and her alert state and documented refusal show a clear informed choice. The emergency exception applies only when the patient's wishes are unknown. Surrogates cannot override a capable patient, and the physician should maximize every acceptable alternative to control bleeding.
  29. The correct answer is B (Low serum iron, high total iron binding capacity, low ferritin, and low transferrin saturation): Iron deficiency depletes stores, so ferritin falls, while the liver makes more transferrin, raising total iron binding capacity and lowering saturation. Anemia of chronic disease shows low iron and low binding capacity with high ferritin because hepcidin traps iron in macrophages, which a normal C-reactive protein argues against. Iron overload and sideroblastic states raise iron and saturation.
  30. The correct answer is B (Flexion and extension as main motion, with sidebending and rotation coupled toward opposite sides): The occipitoatlantal joint is the nodding joint, with flexion and extension as its primary motion and sidebending and rotation coupled to opposite sides. Rotation is the primary motion of C1 on C2, and the same side coupling belongs to typical cervical segments C2 to C7.
  31. The correct answer is A (D2 receptor agonism on lactotrophs, restoring dopamine-mediated inhibition and shrinking the adenoma): Lactotrophs are under tonic dopamine inhibition through D2 receptors, so cabergoline or bromocriptine lowers hormone output and usually shrinks a prolactinoma. Somatostatin analogs are reserved for growth hormone or TSH secreting tumors and work poorly here. Blocking dopamine receptors, as antipsychotics do, would raise the level further.
  32. The correct answer is E (Functional technique, a gentle indirect method following palpated tissue response): In an elderly patient with osteoporosis and a prior vertebral fracture, a gentle indirect method guided by palpated tissue response avoids forceful loading of the spine. Thrust, repetitive springing, strong contraction and firm direct myofascial loading all risk further fracture. Functional technique can be done with the patient in a comfortable position.
  33. The correct answer is E (Abductor pollicis longus and extensor pollicis brevis, which share the tight first dorsal extensor compartment): A positive Finkelstein test with radial styloid tenderness in a new mother points to De Quervain tenosynovitis of the abductor pollicis longus and extensor pollicis brevis. These tendons run in the first dorsal compartment. The second compartment tendons are involved in intersection syndrome, which causes pain proximal to the wrist.
  34. The correct answer is D (Neisseria gonorrhoeae, a gram negative diplococcus that disseminates hematogenously from a mucosal site): Young sexually active adults with migratory polyarthralgia, tenosynovitis, pustular dermatitis and a negative Gram stain have disseminated gonococcal infection, because Neisseria gonorrhoeae is hard to see in joint fluid. Staphylococcus aureus is the most common cause of septic arthritis overall but rarely produces this triad. Rheumatic fever causes a migratory arthritis with a sterile, much less cellular effusion.
  35. The correct answer is D (Tumor production of parathyroid hormone related peptide, which activates receptors in bone and kidney): Keratinizing squamous carcinoma commonly causes humoral hypercalcemia through parathyroid hormone related peptide, which mimics the hormone at its receptor and lowers phosphorus. The suppressed hormone level excludes ectopic hormone and primary hyperparathyroidism. Antacid use can raise calcium, but the mass and low phosphorus point to the tumor mediator.
  36. The correct answer is A (Cervical high velocity low amplitude thrust with rotation, given suspected vertebrobasilar insufficiency): Transient dizziness, diplopia and slurred speech with head turning suggest vertebrobasilar insufficiency, an absolute contraindication to cervical HVLA. Gentle indirect or low-force techniques such as counterstrain, functional and suboccipital release do not stress the vertebral arteries in this way. Light muscle energy kept in neutral is also lower risk.
  37. The correct answer is D (Thoracic lymphatic pump with rhythmic compression across the lower rib cage on both sides): Rhythmic compression of the lower rib cage is unsafe over fresh rib fractures because it can displace the fragments and injure the underlying lung. The other techniques apply no pressure to the injured ribs, and a pedal pump in particular can still assist lymphatic return in pneumonia.
  38. The correct answer is B (Prevalence rises while incidence remains stable, because patients remain in the diseased pool for longer): Prevalence is approximately incidence multiplied by average disease duration. Longer survival without cure extends duration, so existing cases pile up and prevalence rises. Incidence counts only new diagnoses and therefore does not move.
  39. The correct answer is C (Acute hepatocellular injury with preserved synthetic function and mild secondary conjugated hyperbilirubinemia): Aminotransferases above 1,000 with ALT greater than AST, a nearly normal alkaline phosphatase and normal albumin and INR indicate acute hepatocellular necrosis, as in acute viral hepatitis. The direct fraction predominates because injured hepatocytes cannot excrete conjugated bilirubin efficiently. Alcoholic hepatitis rarely pushes AST past a few hundred and reverses the ratio.
  40. The correct answer is B (Counterstrain, an indirect technique held in the position of greatest comfort): A discrete tender point treated by passively positioning the tissue until tenderness drops, then holding about ninety seconds, is counterstrain. Facilitated positional release adds a facilitating force that was not used here. Functional technique has no tender point endpoint, and muscle energy is active and direct.
  41. The correct answer is A (Toe brachial index, because digital arteries rarely develop the medial calcification of ankle vessels): An ankle brachial index above 1.4 reflects medial calcification that prevents compression, which is common in diabetes, so the value cannot be trusted. Digital arteries are usually spared, so the toe brachial index measures perfusion reliably. Inflating the cuff higher does not help, and the other studies address different problems.
  42. The correct answer is C (Delayed hypersensitivity in which sensitized T lymphocytes recognize hapten-modified self proteins): Allergic contact dermatitis from nickel is a type IV, T cell mediated delayed hypersensitivity in which nickel acts as a hapten that binds self proteins. The 24 to 72 hour onset and positive patch test distinguish it from IgE-mediated urticaria. Irritant dermatitis occurs without sensitization and does not give a positive patch test.
  43. The correct answer is E (Incomplete closure of the posterior neuropore near the end of the fourth embryonic week): The caudal opening of the neural tube, the posterior neuropore, normally closes by about day 28, and failure produces myelomeningocele with the elevated alpha-fetoprotein seen here. The banana-shaped cerebellum reflects the associated Chiari II malformation. Anterior neuropore failure causes anencephaly rather than a lumbosacral lesion.
  44. The correct answer is B (Tear of the posterior horn of the medial meniscus with a displaced fragment): Medial joint line pain, locking, and a click on the McMurray maneuver with external rotation indicate a medial meniscal tear. Internal rotation during the maneuver tests the lateral meniscus. A medial collateral sprain causes valgus laxity without mechanical locking.
  45. The correct answer is B (Glossopharyngeal, vagus, and accessory nerves, along with the origin of the internal jugular vein): The jugular foramen lies between the occiput and temporal bone near the occipitomastoid suture and transmits cranial nerves IX, X, and XI plus the beginning of the internal jugular vein. Compression there can impair vagal function, producing poor suck and feeding difficulty in infants. The hypoglossal nerve exits through its own canal, and the other groups exit elsewhere.
  46. The correct answer is C (The fovea is thin and receives choroidal blood supply, so it shows through ischemic retina): In central retinal artery occlusion the inner retina turns pale from ischemia, while the foveola is very thin and nourished by the choroid, so the underlying red color shows through. A storage disease such as Tay-Sachs produces a similar spot for a different reason, and this older vascular patient with sudden painless loss fits occlusion.
  47. The correct answer is B (Give intravenous fibrinolytic therapy now, then transfer for angiography once he is stabilized): In STEMI presenting within 12 hours, reperfusion should not wait for biomarkers. When primary PCI cannot be performed within about 120 minutes of first contact, fibrinolysis is preferred, followed by transfer for angiography. Waiting for troponin or relying on medical therapy alone leaves the artery occluded.
  48. The correct answer is E (Embolic occlusion of the inferior division of the left middle cerebral artery): Fluent speech with poor comprehension is Wernicke aphasia from the posterior superior temporal gyrus, supplied by the inferior division of the middle cerebral artery. Meyer loop fibers in the temporal lobe pass through the same region, producing a contralateral superior quadrantanopia. Superior division lesions cause nonfluent speech with weakness, and an isolated arcuate lesion spares comprehension.
  49. The correct answer is C (Relaxin loosens pelvic ligaments, so large-amplitude thrust techniques at these joints warrant caution): Relaxin from the corpus luteum and placenta softens the pubic symphysis and pelvic ligaments in preparation for delivery. Hypermobile joints are vulnerable to overstretching, so gentle techniques such as muscle energy or counterstrain are favored over large-amplitude thrust. Gentle indirect methods remain safe in pregnancy.
  50. The correct answer is D (Pulmonary embolism from deep venous thrombosis after orthopedic surgery and interrupted prophylaxis): Sudden dyspnea, pleuritic pain, tachycardia, hypoxemia, and clear lungs with a swollen calf several days after hip surgery and interrupted anticoagulation point to pulmonary embolism from deep venous thrombosis. Atelectasis and pneumonia would produce crackles or fever, and fat embolism and edema lack the skin, neurologic, or cardiac features.
  51. The correct answer is A (Phosphodiesterase 5 inhibition prolongs cGMP action, and added nitric oxide donation risks profound hypotension): Phosphodiesterase 5 inhibitors block breakdown of cGMP, so nitric oxide released during stimulation causes sustained cavernosal vasodilation. Nitrates also raise cGMP by donating nitric oxide, so the combination can cause profound, life-threatening hypotension. The two must not be used together.
  52. The correct answer is E (About 93 percent, derived from one minus the inverse of R0, the transmission multiplier): The herd immunity threshold equals one minus one divided by R0. With R0 of 15 this is 1 minus 0.067, or about 93 percent. Using the inverse alone gives 7 percent, and the lower thresholds belong to diseases with a smaller reproduction number.
  53. The correct answer is B (Hypokalemia from loop diuretic use, increasing digoxin binding to the sodium potassium pump): Digoxin binds the potassium site of the sodium potassium ATPase, so low potassium increases binding and toxicity. Loop diuretics commonly cause hypokalemia, and hypomagnesemia and hypercalcemia also potentiate toxicity. Atrial tachycardia with block and scooped ST segments is characteristic.
  54. The correct answer is D (Steroid 5-alpha-reductase type 2 deficiency, sparing wolffian structures but impairing external genital masculinization before puberty): Ambiguous genitalia at birth, normal male testosterone, low dihydrotestosterone and virilization at puberty indicate 5-alpha-reductase 2 deficiency. Dihydrotestosterone is needed for external genital masculinization in utero, while testosterone from the testes later drives pubertal changes. Complete androgen insensitivity would not virilize.
  55. The correct answer is C (Failure of the herniated midgut loops to return to the abdominal cavity after physiologic herniation): Omphalocele is a membrane-covered midline herniation that results when midgut loops that normally herniate into the umbilical cord fail to return by about week 12, and the trisomy 18 features fit. Gastroschisis, by contrast, is a full-thickness defect lateral to the cord with no covering sac and few associated syndromes.
  56. The correct answer is A (Displacement, redirecting anger from the threatening source onto a safer and less powerful target): Displacement redirects an impulse from a threatening target, the supervisor, to a safer substitute, his wife. Projection assigns one's own feelings to others, and reaction formation turns a feeling into its opposite. Passive aggression would be directed at the supervisor himself rather than at a bystander.
  57.
